How Hard is Weak-Memory Testing? Journal: PACMPL Volume: 1 POPL 1 1 CCS: Software and its engineering Software verification and validation CCS: Theory of computation Theory and algorithms for application domains CCS: Theory of computation Program analysis
Soham Chakraborty, Shankaranarayanan Krishna, Umang Mathur, Andreas Pavlogiannis

Abstract
. Weak-memory models are standard formal specifications of concurrency across hardware, programming languages, and distributed systems. A fundamental computational problem is consistency testing : is the observed execution of a concurrent program in alignment with the specification of the underlying system? The problem has been studied extensively across Sequential Consistency (SC) and weak memory, and proven to be NP -complete when some aspect of the input (e.g., number of threads/memory locations) is unbounded. This unboundedness has left a natural question open: are there efficient parameterized algorithms for testing? The main contribution of this paper is a deep hardness result for consistency testing under many popular weak-memory models: the problem remains NP -complete even in its bounded setting, where candidate executions contain a bounded number of threads, memory locations, and values. This hardness spreads across several Release-Acquire variants of C11, a popular variant of its Relaxed fragment, popular Causal Consistency models, and the POWER architecture. To our knowledge, this is the first result that fully exposes the hardness of weak-memory testing and proves that the problem admits no parameterization under standard input parameters. It also yields a computational separation of these models from SC, x86-TSO, PSO, and Relaxed, for which bounded consistency testing is either known (for SC), or shown here (for the rest), to be in polynomial time.



1

1.
Introduction

Memory-consistency models play a crucial role in the design, use, and verification of concurrent systems spanning hardware, programming languages, and distributed computing.
These models formally define the set of behaviors that the system can exhibit as a whole, accounting for the intricate communication patterns between its entities due to buffers, caching, message delays, etc.
The simplest and most widespread, general model is Sequential Consistency (
\mathsf{SC}
)
(
Lamport 1978
)
, which defines program behavior by thread interleaving.
Although its simplicity is a big advantage,
\mathsf{SC}
fails to capture the additional, complex behaviors that are abundant in modern concurrency.

In contrast,
weak-memory models
are richer and more faithful specifications of concurrent/distributed communication and are developed specifically for the system under consideration.
For example, the x86, POWER, and ARM architectures follow their own memory models
(
Owens 2010
;
Alglave et al. 2014
;
Alglave et al. 2021
)
, programming languages provide certain primitives for writing weak-memory concurrent programs
(
Batty et al. 2013
)
, and distributed systems implement various models of causal consistency
(
Hutto and Ahamad 1990
;
Burckhardt 2014
;
Bouajjani et al. 2017
)
.
Naturally, verification techniques are specific to the memory model at hand, so as to account for (and verify) all the possible behaviors that the system can exhibit according to the model.

One of the core computational problems associated with a memory model is that of
consistency testing: is a high-level, observed behavior of a program in alignment with the semantics of the underlying model

(
Gibbons and Korach 1997
)
?
The observed behavior is specified in terms of an abstract execution that defines the sequence of instructions each process/thread executed, the shared memory locations it read from/wrote to, along with the respective values read/written.
Answering this question requires determining the low-level, unobserved behavior of the architecture that gave rise to the observed behavior of the program;
for example, the order in which writes were made visible to (one or more of) the threads, and the dataflow between writes and reads.

Consistency testing is a natural task in both the development and the implementation of memory models.
In particular, memory models are contracts between the designers of a system and its users
(
Adve and Hill 1990
)
.
When designing hardware architectures, memory subsystems, compiler optimizations, and distributed-communication protocols,
consistency-testing serves to validate that the contract has been respected
(
Gibbons and Korach 1997
;
Qadeer 2003
;
Manovit and Hangal 2006
;
Chen et al. 2009
;
Windsor et al. 2022
)
.
From the opposite direction, litmus testing is a standard approach to understanding the semantics of hardware architectures
(
Alglave et al. 2011
;
Alglave et al. 2014
)
so as to design faithful models around them.
Here, given a candidate memory model and the observed execution of a litmus test, consistency checking verifies whether the execution is a counterexample to the model.
Finally, consistency testing is also used as a separability criterion between different memory models
(
Wickerson et al. 2017
;
Kokologiannakis et al. 2023
)
.

Consistency checks are also a widespread task in program verification and testing.
In stateless model checking, the goal of the model checker is to enumerate-and-check the absence of errors in all program executions (typically up to some bound).
To reduce the load of the verification task, an abstraction mechanism partitions the space of all behaviors into equivalence classes, each represented by an abstract execution.
Instead of enumerating concrete executions, the model checker enumerates abstract executions, which yields an exponential reduction of the search space.
Each candidate abstract execution undergoes a consistency check to ensure that the model checker does not diverge to unrealizable parts of the search space
(
Chalupa et al. 2017
;
Abdulla et al. 2018
;
Chatterjee et al. 2019
;
Kokologiannakis et al. 2019
;
Kokologiannakis et al. 2022
;
Abdulla et al. 2019
;
Agarwal et al. 2021
;
Bui et al. 2021
;
Abdulla et al. 2023
)
.
In runtime testing, predictive techniques aim to infer the presence of unobserved, erroneous executions from observed executions that are bug-free.
Such techniques operate by constructing a candidate execution that manifests the bug (using the observed execution as a guide), and then apply a consistency check (explicitly or implicitly) to verify that the execution indeed represents valid program behavior (hence the bug report is a true positive)
(
Kalhauge and Palsberg 2018
;
Huang et al. 2014
;
Pavlogiannis 2019
;
Mathur et al. 2020
;
Luo and Demsky 2021
;
Kini et al. 2017
;
Mathur et al. 2021
)
.

Owing to the widespread applicability, the computational complexity of
consistency testing has been studied thoroughly for a wide variety of memory models
and a wide variety of settings.
The seminal work of
Gibbons and Korach 1997
showed that the problem is
NP
-complete for
\mathsf{SC}
, even when either the number of threads or the number of memory locations is bounded (but not both).
Later,
Cantin et al. 2005
proved that the problem remains
NP
-complete even with a single memory location (but with unboundedly many threads), which also implies
NP
-completness for all memory models that adhere to the “
\mathsf{SC}
-per-location” property, such as
\mathsf{TSO}
,
\mathsf{PSO}
,
\mathsf{RA}
,
\mathsf{SRA}
and
\mathsf{Relaxed}
.

Gonthmakher et al. 2003
showed a similar
NP
-completeness for a Java memory model.

Furbach et al. 2015
proposed a unified treatment of the consistency problem on many weak-memory models which lead to similar
NP
-completeness results,
while the
NP
-completeness of consistency testing under various causal-consistency models was proven in
(
Bouajjani et al. 2017
)
.

A popular approach to tackle the intractability in consistency testing is via
parameterization
: an intractable problem becomes tractable when some of its input parameters, such as the number of threads, is bounded
(
Gibbons and Korach 1994
;
Mathur et al. 2020
;
Abdulla et al. 2018
)
.
On the other hand, all existing results that establish the
NP
-hardness of consistency testing in all memory models rely on some input parameters being unbounded.
For
\mathsf{SC}
, this unboundedness is, in fact, a prerequisite for intractability: the problem becomes polynomial-time when both the number of threads and memory locations are bounded, a result that has led to efficient parameterized model checking
(
Agarwal et al. 2021
)
.
For weak-memory, however, analogous results have thus far remained elusive.
In particular, are there any efficient parameterized algorithms for consistency in weak memories?

How hard, actually, is weak-memory testing?

Here we resolve this question by establishing a deep hardness result for many popular weak-memory models: consistency testing is
NP
-complete even in its
bounded
setting,
where executions contain a constant number of threads, memory locations and values, and the size of the input is solely determined by the (unbounded) number of events.
To our knowledge, this is the first result that fully exposes the hardness of weak-memory testing and proves that the problem admits no parameterization under standard input parameters.
In turn, this implies that practical approaches to testing have to resort to heuristics, while model checkers might be more performant when exploring finer abstractions (such as reads-from
(
Chalupa et al. 2017
;
Abdulla et al. 2019
;
Tunç et al. 2023
)
, or those based on executions graphs
(
Kokologiannakis et al. 2017
;
Lahav and Margalit 2019
)
).

Our contributions.

We study the
bounded consistency testing problem
for many popular weak-memory models found in software, hardware, and distributed systems.
The input is always an abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po})
consisting of a set of events
\mathsf{E}
and a program order
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
defining the order of execution of these events in each thread.
The task is to determine whether
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
is consistent in a given memory model.
The
boundedness
of the problem refers to the number of threads, memory locations, and values accessed by
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
being bounded (i.e., constant).
It is easy to see that the problem is in
NP
in all the models we consider, and we will not be establishing this fact formally.
We write
\mathcal{M}_{1}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{M}_{2}
to denote that memory model
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is weaker than memory model
\mathcal{M}_{1}
, i.e., any execution that is consistent in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is also consistent in
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.

We begin with release-acquire semantics, as popularized by C11.
We consider the Release-Acquire model (
\mathsf{RA}
), as well as its Strong (
\mathsf{SRA}
) and Weak variants (
\mathsf{WRA}
)
(
Lahav and Boker 2022
)
.
In addition, we consider
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
, the standard Relaxed semantics of C11 equipped with the common assumption of causal acyclicity (aka
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
acyclicity).
This assumption is often used as an additional axiom
(
Margalit and Lahav 2021
;
Norris and Demsky 2013
)
as it has been argued that
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
-cycles do not arise in practice
(
Lee et al. 2023
)
.
We prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1.

Consistency testing for bounded inputs is
NP
-complete for
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
as well as
for any memory model
\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathsf{SRA}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{M}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{WRA}
, even in their atomic read-modify-write (RMW)-free fragment.

Note that
Theorem

1.1
establishes hardness for
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
,
\mathsf{WRA}
,
\mathsf{RA}
,
\mathsf{SRA}
as well as the whole range of models between
\mathsf{SRA}
and
\mathsf{WRA}
.
This result improves existing results on consistency checking, for which hardness relied on an unbounded domain of threads and/or memory locations
(
Gibbons and Korach 1994
;
Cantin et al. 2005
;
Furbach et al. 2015
)
.

Next, we turn our attention to popular causal-consistency models
(
Lamport 1978
;
Fidge 1988
)
.
There have been several efforts to formalize various aspects of causal consistency,
out of which have emerged three well-accepted models, namely
Causal Consistency
\mathsf{CC}

(
Hutto and Ahamad 1990
;
Bouajjani et al. 2017
)
,
Causal Convergence
\mathsf{CCv}

(
Burckhardt 2014
;
Perrin et al. 2016
;
Bouajjani et al. 2017
)
, and
Causal Memory
\mathsf{CM}

(
Ahamad et al. 1995
;
Perrin et al. 2016
;
Bouajjani et al. 2017
)
.
It was recently shown that
\mathsf{CC}
coincides with
\mathsf{WRA}
while
\mathsf{CCv}
coincides with
\mathsf{SRA}

(
Lahav and Boker 2022
)
.
Thus
Theorem

1.1
extends to
\mathsf{CC}
and
\mathsf{CCv}
.
We prove that the problem is also hard for
\mathsf{CM}
, thereby establishing hardness for the ranges defined by the three main models.

\mathsf{SC}
\mathsf{TSO}
\mathsf{CM}
\mathsf{PSO}
\mathsf{SRA}=\mathsf{CCv}
\mathsf{RA}
\mathsf{WRA}=\mathsf{CC}
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
\mathsf{Relaxed}
\mathsf{POWER}
P
P
NP
-complete

Figure 1
.

The complexity landscape of bounded weak-memory testing.
An arrow
\mathcal{M}_{1}\to\mathcal{M}_{2}
means that
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is stronger than
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.
Thick arrows represent range-hardness for all models between the endpoints.
The complexity of bounded consistency checking for all models except for
\mathsf{SC}
are established in this paper.

Theorem 1.2.

Consistency testing for bounded inputs is
NP
-complete for any memory model
\mathcal{M}
such that
(i)
\mathsf{CCv}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{M}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{CC}
or
(ii)
\mathsf{CM}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{M}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{CC}
.

Next, we turn our attention to the
\mathsf{POWER}
architecture.

Lahav et al. 2016
show that
\mathsf{SRA}
captures precisely the guarantees of
\mathsf{POWER}
for programs that are compiled from the release-acquire fragment of C/C++.
Thus
Theorem

1.1
extends to the following corollary.

Corollary 1.3.

Consistency testing for bounded inputs is
NP
-complete for
\mathsf{POWER}
.

Continuing with hardware models, we study Total Store Order
(\mathsf{TSO})
as employed in x86 architectures (aka x86-TSO) and its extension to Partial Store Order
(\mathsf{PSO})
.
It turns out that, in the bounded setting, consistency checks become tractable in these models.

Theorem 1.4.

Consistency testing for bounded threads and memory locations is in polynomial time for
\mathsf{TSO}
and
\mathsf{PSO}
.

One natural, final question concerns the vanilla
\mathsf{Relaxed}
model, i.e., if we remove the acyclicity condition from
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
.
In this case, the problem becomes polynomial-time, which is a corollary of the corresponding result for
\mathsf{SC}

(
Agarwal et al. 2021
)
.

Corollary 1.5.

Consistency testing for bounded threads is in polynomial time for
\mathsf{Relaxed}
.

Although
Corollary

1.5
is technically straightforward, it is conceptually interesting under the following realization.
For all of our previous results (as well as for
\mathsf{SC}
), the hardness of consistency coincides with whether the corresponding model exhibits multi-copy atomicity.
In contrast,
\mathsf{Relaxed}
is non-multi-copy atomic, yet consistency testing is in polynomial time.

Following the results of this paper,
Fig.

1
pictorially presents the full landscape of the tractability and the hardness in testing weak memories.

High-level intuition.

Our proofs exploit complex combinatorial properties that arise in weak memory.
Although it is hard to pinpoint one key insight that fully explains our hardness results,
our proofs rely on the fact that most of the models we consider
(i) are causally consistent, and
(ii) allow
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}fr})
-cycles,
where
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}^{\prime}
is the standard program order restricted to instructions of the same type (read-read and write-write orderings) on different locations.
In contrast, the polynomial-time models
\mathsf{SC}
and
\mathsf{TSO}
forbid (ii), while
\mathsf{PSO}
allows (ii) but also fails (i).

Outline.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.

•

In
Section

2
, we define our problem setting and the memory models we consider based on C/C++ atomics.
We also develop relevant notation that will be helpful in later sections.

•

In
Section

3
, we prove
Theorem

1.1
for
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
.
For readability, we prove a weaker version of
Theorem

1.1
in which the inputs use boundedly many threads and locations but manipulate unboundedly many values.
Later in
Section

6
, we explain how to perform simple modifications to our reduction to make it work even for bounded values.

•

In
Section

4
, we prove
Theorem

1.1
for all models
\mathsf{SRA}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{M}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{WRA}
.
Similarly to the previous case, our reduction uses unboundedly many values, while the modifications described in
Section

6
also apply to this model, to arrive at the final result.

•

In
Section

5
, we establish
Theorem

1.2
,
Corollary

1.3
,
Theorem

1.4
and
Corollary

1.5
.

•

Finally, in
Section

6
, we present the modifications in the reductions of
Section

3
and
Section

4
that fully establish
Theorem

1.1
.

Some proofs are relegated to the Appendix.

2.
Preliminaries

This section defines the axiomatic semantics of the
\mathsf{SRA}
,
\mathsf{RA}
,
\mathsf{WRA}
, and
\mathsf{Relaxed}
memory models.
As these are standard concepts, our exposition follows recent work on the topic (e.g.,
(
Margalit and Lahav 2021
;
Lahav and Boker 2022
;
Tunç et al. 2023
)
).
In axiomatic semantics, program executions consist of sets of events and relations between them.
Given an integer
i
, we let
[i]=\{1,2,\dots,i\}
.

Events.

An event is a tuple
\langle\mathsf{id},\mathsf{tid},\mathsf{lab}\rangle
where
\mathsf{id}
,
\mathsf{tid}
,
\mathsf{lab}
denote a unique identifier, thread identifier, and the label respectively.
The label is of the form
\mathsf{lab}=\langle\mathsf{op},\mathsf{loc},\mathsf{Val},\mathsf{ord}\rangle
where
\mathsf{op}
,
\mathsf{loc}
,
\mathsf{Val}
,
\mathsf{ord}
respectively denote a read (
\mathtt{r}
) or write (
\mathtt{w}
) memory operation, accessed memory location, read or written value, and memory order respectively.
For the
\mathsf{SRA}
,
\mathsf{RA}
, and
\mathsf{WRA}
models, reads and writes are of
acquire
and
release
orders respectively.
For the
\mathsf{Relaxed}
model, the read and write accesses have
relaxed
order.
These memory orders are used to define the semantics of models like C11, but we will not be using them explicitly here.
As we treat each model separately, all access orders are determined by the models and are never mixed.
Hence, we will simply write
\mathtt{r}(t,x,v)
/
\mathtt{w}(t,x,v)
to denote a read/write event of thread
t
, accessing location
x
and reading/writing value
v
.
We occasionally omit
x
and/or
v
, when it is irrelevant or clear from the context, while we let
\mathsf{tid}(e)
denote the thread of event
e
.
We do not introduce fences or atomic read-modify-write (RMW) events, as all our hardness results hold even with only read/write events, while our positive results can be easily extended to handle fences and RMWs.
Finally, we denote the set of read and write accesses by
\mathsf{R}
and
\mathsf{W}
respectively.

Notation on relations.

Let
B
be a binary relation over a set of events
\mathsf{E}
.
The reflexive, transitive, reflexive-transitive closures, and inverse relations of
B

are denoted as
B^{?}
,
B^{+}
,
B^{*}
, and
B^{-1}
, respectively.
We compose two relations
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
as
B_{1};B_{2}
.

[A]
denotes the identity relation on a set
A
.
We write
\mathsf{irr}(B)
and
\mathsf{acy}(B)
to denote that
B
is irreflexive and acyclic, respectively.
We occasionally write that there exists a
B
-edge
e\xrightarrow{B}e^{\prime}
to denote that
(e,e^{\prime})\in B
.
We naturally extend this notation to paths, so that a
B
-path
P\colon e\mathrel{\hbox to11.49pt{\vbox to10.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.74426pt\lower-5.64442pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

is a sequence of
B
-edges

e=e_{1}\xrightarrow{B}e_{2}\xrightarrow{B}\cdots\xrightarrow{B}e_{i}=e^{\prime}
.
Finally, we write
B_{x}
to restrict
B
on events accessing location
x
.

Executions and relations.

An execution is a tuple
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=\langle\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}\rangle
where
\mathsf{E}
is a set of events and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}
are binary relations over
\mathsf{E}
.
In particular, the
program order
(
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\subseteq(\mathsf{E}\times\mathsf{E})
) is a strict total order on the events of each thread.
The
reads-from
relation (
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}\subseteq(\mathsf{W}\times\mathsf{R})
) relates a write and read event pair
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r})
,
denoting that
\mathtt{r}
obtains its value from
\mathtt{w}
.
Every read reads from exactly one write on the same memory location and having the same value (thus
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}^{-1}
is a function).
The
modification order
(
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}\subseteq\bigcup_{x}(\mathsf{W}_{x}\times\mathsf{W}_{x})
) is a strict total order over
same-location writes in an execution.
Finally, the
happens-before
relation is defined as
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\triangleq(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
.

Fig.

2
shows examples of executions presented as
execution graphs
.
In each execution graph the nodes represent events and the edges represent relations.
We omit some relation-edges that are clear from the context

*
*

*

It is also common to define a
from reads
relation
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}fr}\triangleq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}^{-1};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}
.
However, we will not be using
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}fr}
explicitly in this paper.
.

Consistency Axioms.

Consistency axioms capture different aspects or properties of an execution, such as coherence and causality cycles, under a memory model.
These properties are interpreted differently in different memory models.

Coherence.

In an execution,
coherence
enforces an ordering between same-location events.
For events using the release-acquire memory orders,

write-coherence
requires that each
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{x}
order agrees with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
.
A stronger variant is
strong-write-coherence
, which requires that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}
agrees with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, transitively.

Read coherence
enforces that a read
\mathtt{r}
can read from a write
\mathtt{w}
when
there is no
intermediate
write
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
on the same-location that happens-before
\mathtt{r}
.
Depending upon how “intermediate” writes are treated,
two variations of read coherence are
popular — in standard
read-coherence
,

\mathtt{w}
and
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
are ordered by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{x}
whereas in
weak-read-coherence
they are ordered by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}_{x}
.
Finally, we also have variants of write and read coherence when all accesses are relaxed.
Here
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
is replaced with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
, as
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edges do not contribute to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
between different memory locations.

Table 1
.

Variants of the coherence axioms.

Release-Acquire

Relaxed

\mathsf{irr}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{x};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb})

(write-coherence)

\mathsf{irr}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{x};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po})

(relaxed-write-coherence)

\mathsf{acy}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})

(strong-write-coherence)

\mathsf{irr}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}^{-1};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{x};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb})

(read-coherence)

\mathsf{irr}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}^{-1};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{x};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}^{?};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po})

(relaxed-read-coherence)

\mathsf{irr}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}_{x};[\mathsf{W}];\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}_{x};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}^{-1})

(weak-read-coherence)

Causality Cycles.

A causality cycle arises in the presence of relaxed accesses and consists of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
orderings.
A causality cycle may result in ‘out-of-thin-air’ behavior in an execution.
To avoid such ‘out-of-thin-air’ behavior, many consistency models and verification tools explicitly disallow such cycles
(
Norris and Demsky 2013
;
Margalit and Lahav 2021
;
Luo and Demsky 2021
)
.

•

(porf-acyclicity)

\mathtt{w}(y)
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}

\mathtt{r}(y)

(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{x};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb})
.

is forbidden by strong-write-coherence.
The execution in

(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}^{-1};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{x};\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb})
and thus fails read-coherence.
In

, we have

,
(\mathtt{w}(x),\mathtt{r}(x))\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\subseteq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, and
(\mathtt{r}(x),\mathtt{w}(x))\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}^{-1}
,
violating weak-read-coherence.
The execution in
Fig.

2(e)
violates porf-acyclicity.
Finally, the executions in
Fig.

2(f)
and
Fig.

2(g)
violate relaxed-write-coherence and relaxed-read-coherence, respectively.

Memory Models.

We can now describe the main memory models we consider in this work, by listing the axioms that each execution needs to satisfy in the respective model
Table

2
.

Release-Acquire and variants.

The release-acquire (
\mathsf{RA}
) memory model is weaker than sequential consistency and is arguably the most well-understood fragment of C11.
Here, the reads-from relation
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
induces synchronization between thread threads, which is captured in the semantics by the happens-before relation
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
.
Following
(
Lahav and Boker 2022
)
, we consider three variants of
release-acquire models: Release-Acquire (
\mathsf{RA}
), and its Strong (
\mathsf{SRA}
) and Weak (
\mathsf{WRA}
) variants.

\mathsf{SRA}
enforces strong-write-coherence on write accesses whereas
\mathsf{RA}
enforces write-coherence.
On the other hand,
\mathsf{WRA}
does not place any ordering between same-location writes by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{x}
.
Instead, the only orderings considered between same-location writes are through the
[\mathsf{W}];\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}_{x};[\mathsf{W}]
relation.

Table 2
.

The main weak-memory models based on C11 that we consider in this work.

Release-Acquire

Relaxed

\mathsf{WRA}

porf-acyclicity

weak-read-coherence

\mathsf{Relaxed}

relaxed-write-coherence

relaxed-read-coherence

\mathsf{RA}

write-coherence

read-coherence

\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}

relaxed-write-coherence

relaxed-read-coherence

porf-acyclicity

\mathsf{SRA}

strong-write-coherence

read-coherence

Relaxed.

All accesses in the
\mathsf{Relaxed}
model
satisfy the corresponding coherence axioms relaxed-write-coherence and relaxed-read-coherence, which guarantee
\mathsf{SC}
-per-location.
The
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
model strengthens
\mathsf{Relaxed}
by also requiring the acyclicity of
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
.

Based on the set of allowed behaviors, these models can be partially ordered as
\mathsf{SRA}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{RA}\preccurlyeq\{\mathsf{WRA},\{\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{Relaxed}\}\}
, where models towards the right allow more behaviors.

The consistency-testing problem.

An execution
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
is
consistent
in a memory model
\mathcal{M}
, written
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\models\mathcal{M}
, if it satisfies the axioms of
\mathcal{M}
.
For example, the execution in
Fig.

2(b)
satisfies all axioms except strong-write-coherence, and hence it is consistent in
\mathsf{RA}
,
\mathsf{WRA}
, and
\mathsf{Relaxed(\text{-}Acyclic)}
.

When testing the behavior of a program within a memory model, one does not have access to
concrete
executions, but rather to
abstract
executions.
The latter contains only information observed by the program, i.e., the events it executed and the values it read/wrote.
Formally, an
abstract execution

\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}=\langle\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\rangle
is a coarser object than concrete executions, missing the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
relations, and a concrete execution

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=\langle\mathsf{E}^{\prime},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}^{\prime},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}^{\prime},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}^{\prime}\rangle
is said to be an extension of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
if
\mathsf{E}^{\prime}=\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}^{\prime}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
.
We call
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}

consistent
in
\mathcal{M}
, written similarly as
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathcal{M}
, if there exists an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}
such that the extension
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=\langle\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}\rangle
is an execution with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\models\mathcal{M}
.
The problem of
consistency testing
in a memory model
\mathcal{M}
is to determine whether
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
is consistent in
\mathcal{M}
,
i.e., whether there is a way to resolve
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}
in
\mathcal{M}
that would give rise to the observed behavior
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
on the program level.

Conflicting triplets.

In the coming sections, we use the notion of
conflicting triplets
.
Given an abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po})
, we say that two events
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}

conflict
if they access the same location and at least one of them is a write.
Given additionally a reads-from relation
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
a
conflicting triplet
(or
triplet
, for short) is a tuple
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
of pairwise conflicting events such that
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.

3.
Hardness for Relaxed-Acyclic

We start with the
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
memory model and show that consistency testing is
NP
-complete under bounded threads and memory locations.
This differs slightly from
Theorem

1.1
, which states that the problem remains hard even with bounded values.
Since our proof is rather technical, we choose to present this intermediate result here.
We will make the final step towards
Theorem

1.1
in
Section

6
, which consists of a simple modification of the technique presented here.
In
Section

3.1
, we present the hardness reduction
and argue about its correctness in
Sections

3.2
and
3.3
.

3.1.
Reduction

Our reduction is from Monotone 1-in-3 SAT which is known to be
NP
-complete
(
Garey and Johnson 1990
)
.
The input is a monotone formula
\varphi
in conjunctive normal form, where each clause contains three literals, all of which are positive.
The task is to determine if there exists a 1-in-3 truth assignment for
\varphi
, i.e., one that
sets exactly one literal to
true
in each clause.

We remark that our reduction is combinatorially elaborate.
We found that complex interactions between threads are necessary to
expose the the nuances that make the consistency problem for the
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
memory model (or for that matter, other memory models we consider) hard.
Nevertheless, we assist the text with illustrations that help visualize and generalize the interaction patterns that are exploited in our reduction.
To further enhance readability, we distinguish different memory locations with different colors (in both figures and main text).

Let
\varphi=\{\mathcal{C}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
be a monotone Boolean formula over
n
variables
\{s_{j}\}_{j\in[n]}
and
m
clauses of the form
\mathcal{C}_{i}=(s_{j},s_{k},s_{\ell})
.
We construct an abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po})
such that
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}

iff

\varphi
is satisfiable using a 1-in-3 assignment
†
†

†

We often use the phrase ‘
\varphi
is satisfiable’ to mean ‘
\varphi
is satisfiable by a 1-in-3 assignment’.
.

t_{1}
t_{2}
t_{3}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{1}_{1})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{1}_{2})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{1}_{n})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{m}_{1})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{m}_{n})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{1}_{1})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{1}_{2})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{1}_{n})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{m}_{1})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{m}_{n})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},v^{1}_{1})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},v^{1}_{2})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},v^{1}_{n})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},v^{m}_{1})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},v^{m}_{n})}

.

The schematic reduction from a monotone formula
\varphi
to an abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
.

High-level description.

Our reduction constructs an abstract execution with
O(n\cdot m)
events
accessing
d=14
memory locations
in
\kappa=23
threads,
of which the three threads
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
and
t_{3}
form the core of the construction.
Events appear in each of these threads in
m

phases
(one phase per clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
), starting from phase
1
and going to larger phases as we go downwards in the threads.
Each phase, in turn, consists of
n

steps
(one step per variable
s_{j}
), again starting from step
1
and going to larger steps as we go downwards.
In phase
i
and step
j
we have a read event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
that can read from either of two writes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
.
The former case corresponds to the assignment
s_{j}=\perp
, while the latter case corresponds to the assignment
s_{j}=\top
.
See
Fig.

3
for an illustration.
Our construction guarantees that the choices for the writer of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
are consistent across all phases
i
: either each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
, which corresponds to setting
s_{j}=\perp
in
\varphi
, or each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
, which corresponds to setting
s_{j}=\top
in
\varphi
.
Moreover, for each clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}=(s_{j},s_{k},s_{\ell})
, our reduction guarantees that exactly one of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})}
reads from thread
t_{2}
, which implies that the corresponding assignment on
s_{j}
,
s_{k}
and
s_{\ell}
satisfies the 1-in-3 property.
To achieve all these constraints, we introduce four
gadgets
, which consist of events on additional threads and memory locations, that guarantee the desired properties.
In the following, we first describe each gadget separately, and then explain how to interleave them in order to obtain the abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
.

The copy gadget
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
(
4(a)
) captures the Boolean assignment to variable
s_{j}
in phase
i
.
There are two ways to realize this gadget, by choosing which of the two writes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
the read
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
observes.
Choosing the write of
t_{1}
(
4(b)
) corresponds to setting
s_{j}=\perp
and also forces
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
to read from
t_{4}
.
Choosing the write of
t_{2}
(
4(c)
) corresponds to setting
s_{j}=\top
and also forces
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
to read from
t_{5}
.
This
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
coupling is formalized in
Lemma

3.1
.
The edge numbers specify the order in which
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edges are inferred.

The copy gadget
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
.

The main gadget in our construction is the copy gadget
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
,
defined for each
i\in[m]
and
j\in[n]
, and shown in
Fig.

4
.
This gadget contains
(i) the three focal events
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
that determine the truth value of
s_{j}
,
(ii) three “mirror” events
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{4},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{5},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
, and
(iii) other events on memory locations

{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0.2578,0.6641}y_{2}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9375,0.5938,0.1758}y_{3}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}y_{4}}
.

The gadget couples the writers of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
: if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{1}
then
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{4}
(see
Fig.

4(b)
), while
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{2}
then
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{5}
(see
Fig.

4(c)
).

The copy-down gadget
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
is very similar to
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
.
Choosing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
to read from
t_{1}
(
5(a)
) corresponds to setting
s_{j}=\perp
and also forces
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
to read from
t_{4}
.
Choosing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
to read from
t_{2}
(
5(b)
) corresponds to setting
s_{j}=\top
and also forces
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
to read from
t_{5}
.
This
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
coupling is formalized in
Lemma

3.1
.
The edge numbers specify the order in which
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edges are inferred.

The copy-down gadget
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
.

We use a copy-down gadget
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
, defined for
i\in[m-1]
and
j\in[n]
, with structure identical to
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
, and shown in
Fig.

5
. This gadget contains
(i) the three focal events
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
,
(ii) the three mirror events
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{4},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{5},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
, and
(iii) other events on memory locations
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0.2578,0.6641}z_{2}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9375,0.5938,0.1758}z_{3}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}z_{4}}
.

While
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
couples the writers of the focal and mirror read events —
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
— belonging to the same phase,

\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
couples the writers of the focal and mirror read events —
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
— of consecutive phases.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{1}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{4}
, while
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{2}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{5}
.
Together,
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
and
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
couple the writers of the focal reads
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
across consecutive phases — either both read from thread
t_{1}
or both read from
t_{2}
.

.

The at-most-one-true gadgets
\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i}_{j,k}
.

\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i}_{j,k}
is shown in

and contains

and

, and

{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}
.
The gadget guarantees that at most one of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
and

t_{2}

.
The edge numbers specify the order in which

The crossed-out events are ignored in our analysis for simplicity (see
Lemma

3.5
).

Putting the gadgets together.

We serially connect all gadgets in their common threads by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
.
In particular,
\operatorname{Copy}^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}
appears before
\operatorname{Copy}^{i_{2}}_{j_{2}}
if
i_{1}<i_{2}
or
i_{1}=i_{2}
and
j_{1}<j_{2}
;

\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}
appears before
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i_{2}}_{j_{2}}
if
i_{1}<i_{2}
or
i_{1}=i_{2}
and
j_{1}<j_{2}
;
each
\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i_{1}}_{j_{1},k_{1}}
appears before
\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i_{2}}_{j_{2},k_{2}}
if
i_{1}<i_{2}
, and finally

\operatorname{ALOne}^{i_{1}}_{j_{1},k_{1},\ell_{1}}
appears before
\operatorname{ALOne}^{i_{2}}_{j_{2},k_{2},\ell_{2}}
if
i_{1}<i_{2}
.
As various gadgets have common threads and events, besides connecting them, we also need to specify the interleaving between them.
However, this interleaving can be arbitrary and we will not fix it here.
Finally, we have indeed used
\kappa=23
threads and
d=14
memory locations.

3.2.
Soundness

We first establish the soundness of the reduction, i.e., if
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
is consistent (using an extension
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
) within
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
, then
\varphi
has a satisfying assignment.
In this direction, we will establish some intermediate lemmas.
Recall that we obtain the satisfying assignment for
\varphi
by assigning
s_{j}=\top
if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
for all
i\in[m]
, and
s_{j}=\perp
if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
for all
i\in[m]
.
The first lemma is based on the copy gadgets
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
and
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
and states that each phase of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
makes consistent choices for the writer of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
(i.e., whether it reads from
t_{1}
or
t_{2}
), which makes the above assignment well-defined.
It follows from the high-level description of these two gadgets and the accompanying
Fig.

4
and
Fig.

5
.

Lemma 3.1.

Let
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})
be a concrete extension of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\models\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
.
For all
i_{1},i_{2}\in[m]
,
j\in[n]
, we have that
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i_{1}}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i_{1}}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
iff
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i_{2}}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i_{2}}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.

Proof.

We argue by induction that for every
i\in[m-1]
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
iff
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
First, note that if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, then the copy gadget
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
forces
({\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{4},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Indeed, we have the following inferred sequence of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
edges (see
1
-
5
in

Fig.

4(b)
, where
1
represents
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
).

(1)

We have
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to porf-acyclicity.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
is forced to read from the only other available write of the same value, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{1},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, depicted as
2
in
Fig.

4(b)
.

(2)

We now have
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{1},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},y_{1},u^{i}_{j})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}}}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}}})^{+}
,
and since
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{2},y_{1},u^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},y_{1},u^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{1},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{2},y_{1},u^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}
due to relaxed-read-coherence.
Observe that now
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{1},y_{1},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{2},y_{1},\overline{u}^{i}_{j})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}}}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}}})^{+}
,
thus due to relaxed-write-coherence, we have

({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{1},y_{1},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{2},y_{1},\overline{u}^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}}}
.
Since
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{2},y_{1},\overline{u}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(f_{3},y_{1},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}}}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}}})^{+}
,
we have
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{1},y_{1},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(f_{3},y_{1},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to relaxed-read-coherence.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(f_{3},y_{1},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(t_{5},y_{1},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(f_{3},y_{1},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, depicted by
3
.

(3)

We now have
({\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{5},y_{4},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}\mathtt{w}(f_{3},y_{4},v^{i}_{j})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}\mathtt{w}(f_{3},y_{4},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{5},y_{4},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, due to porf-acyclicity.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{5},y_{4},v^{i}_{j})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{6},y_{4},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{5},y_{4},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
depicted by
4
.

(4)

We now have
({\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{5},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{5},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to porf-acyclicity.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{4},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
depicted by
5
.

On the other hand, if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, then the copy gadget
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
forces
({\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{5},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, by a similar analysis (see
Fig.

4(c)
, depicted by
1
-
5
).

Finally, a similar analysis on the copy-down gadget
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
(see
Fig.

5
, and the forced
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
edges
1
-
5
) concludes that
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
iff
({\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{4},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
and hence we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
iff
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, as desired.
∎

The next lemma is based on the at-most-one-true gadgets
\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i}_{j,k}
, and it is used to show that for every clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and for each of the three pairs of literals
(s_{j},s_{k})
in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, at most one of them is assigned to true.
Again, it follows by a direct analysis of the accompanying figure,
Fig.

6
.

Lemma 3.2.

Let
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})
be a concrete extension of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\models\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
.
For every
i\in[m]
and
j,k\in[n]

such that
s_{j}
and
s_{k}
appear in clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
or
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.

Proof.

The statement follows by analyzing the at-most-one-true gadget
\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i}_{j,k}
, where
c\in[3]
is such that
(s_{j},s_{k})
is the
c
-th pair of variables in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
(see
Fig.

6
).

First, if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
holds (marked
1
in
Fig.

6(b)
)
then
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Indeed, we have the following sequence of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
edges (see
1
-
4
,
Fig.

6(b)
).

(1)

We have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to porf-acyclicity.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, depicted by
2
.

(2)

We now have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}})^{+}
,
and due to relaxed-write-coherence, we also have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}}
.
In turn, this implies
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to relaxed-read-coherence.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
depicted by
3
.

(3)

We now have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to porf-acyclicity.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
depicted by
4
.

Second, if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
marked

1
in
Fig.

6(c)
),
then
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Indeed, we have the following forced sequence of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
edges (see
Fig.

6(c)
).

(1)

We have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to porf-acyclicity.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, marked
2
.

(2)

Due to relaxed-write-coherence, we have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}}

We thus have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, as this would imply that
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}})
, which would violate relaxed-read-coherence.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
marked
3
.

(3)

We now have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to porf-acyclicity.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
depicted by
4
.
∎

The third lemma is based on the at-least-one-true gadget
\operatorname{ALOne}^{i}_{j,k,\ell}
, and it is used to show that for every clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}=(s_{j},s_{k},s_{\ell})
, at least one of its literals is assigned to true.
Again, it follows by a direct analysis on the accompanying figure,
Fig.

7
.

Lemma 3.3.

Let
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})
be a concrete extension of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\models\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
.
For every
i\in[m]
and
j,k,\ell\in[n]
such that
s_{j}
,
s_{k}
and
s_{\ell}
appear in clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
or
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
or
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.

Proof.

First, if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
(hence both read from
t_{1}
, marked
1
in
Fig.

7(b)
) then
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Indeed, we have the following forced sequence of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
edges (see
Fig.

7(b)
,
1
-
4
).

(1)

We have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{j})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to porf-acyclicity.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{j})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(p,b,v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Similarly, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{k})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to porf-acyclicity.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{k})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(q,b,v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
These two edges are depicted
2
.

(2)

We now have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(p,b,v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}})^{+}
,
and due to relaxed-write-coherence, we also have

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(p,b,v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(p,b,v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}
.
In turn, this implies
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(p,b,v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to relaxed-read-coherence.
Similarly, we now have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(q,b,v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}})^{+}
,
and due to relaxed-write-coherence, we also have

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(q,b,v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(q,b,v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}}
.
In turn, this implies
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(q,b,v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to relaxed-read-coherence.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{\ell})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, depicted
3
.

(3)

We now have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})^{+}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to porf-acyclicity.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
depicted by
4
.

A similar analysis establishes that if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
then
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
(see
Fig.

7(c)
), as well as that if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
then
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
(see
Fig.

7(d)
).
∎

Lemma

3.1
states that our truth assignment for
\varphi
is valid, while
Lemma

3.2
and
Lemma

3.3
guarantee that in every clause, exactly one literal is set to true.
Hence we have the following corollary.

Corollary 3.4.

If
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
then
\varphi
is satisfiable.

3.3.
Completeness

We now turn our attention to the completeness property, i.e., if
\varphi
is satisfiable then
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
is consistent in the
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
model.
To this end, we construct a reads-from relation
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and a partial modification order
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
as follows.

(1)

For each gadget, we insert
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edges according to

Figs.

4
,
5
,
6
and
7

and the truth assignments on literals
s_{j}
,
s_{k}
,
s_{\ell}
involved in that gadget.
In particular, this implies that, for each
i\in[m]
and
j\in[n]
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
if
s_{j}=\perp
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
if
s_{j}=\top
.
Observe that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
is fully specified, i.e., every read has been assigned a write.

(2)

For every two conflicting writes
\mathtt{w}_{1},\mathtt{w}_{2}
with
(i)
\mathsf{tid}(\mathtt{w}_{1})\neq\mathsf{tid}(\mathtt{w}_{2})
, and
(ii) there exist two reads
\mathtt{r}_{1}
,
\mathtt{r}_{2}
with
(\mathtt{r}_{1},\mathtt{r}_{2})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
such that
(\mathtt{w}_{i},\mathtt{r}_{i})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
for each
i\in[2]
,
we have
(\mathtt{w}_{1},\mathtt{w}_{2})\in\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
.

We call a triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
on location
x

safe
in
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
if either
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\not\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x})^{+}
or
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})^{+}
.
To prove the consistency of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
it suffices to prove the following:

(1)

(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
is acyclic, and

(2)

\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
is
minimally coherent
for
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
, namely, that
(i)
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})
is acyclic for every location
x
, and
(ii) every triplet is safe.

Indeed, minimal-coherence guarantees that, for each location
x
, there exists a total extension
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}_{x}
of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x}
that satisfies relaxed-write-coherence and relaxed-read-coherence
(
Tunç et al. 2023
)
.

To simplify our analysis, we ignore the writes in threads
\{h_{c}\}_{c\in[3]}
,
p
and
q
that are not read in
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
(crossed-out in
Fig.

6
and
Fig.

7
).
This allows us to make our formal statements more uniform, while this omission does not affect the correctness of the analysis.
Indeed, let

\operatorname{InactiveWr}=\{\mathtt{w}\in\mathsf{W}\,|\,\mathsf{tid}(\mathtt{w})\in\{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},p,q\}\text{ and }\not\exists\mathtt{r}\in\mathsf{R}.~(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}\}
.
The following lemma is straightforward.

Lemma 3.5.

The following statements hold.

(1)

If there is a
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
-cycle then there is such a cycle without any write in
\operatorname{InactiveWr}
.

(2)

If there is a
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})
-cycle for some memory location
x
then there is such a cycle without any write in
\operatorname{InactiveWr}
.

(3)

If there is an unsafe triplet, then there is such a triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
where
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\not\in\operatorname{InactiveWr}
.

Proof.

We prove each item separately.

(1)

We first observe that for any
\mathtt{w}\in\operatorname{InactiveWr}
, there is no outgoing
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
or even
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
edge.
Hence any cycle containing a write
\mathtt{w}\in\operatorname{InactiveWr}
must contain a sequence of edges
e_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}}{\mathtt{w}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}}e_{2}
, which can be replaced by the single edge
e_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}}e_{2}
.

(2)

Proof same as above.

(3)

Consider a memory location
x
and an unsafe triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
on
x
.
This means that there is a
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x})
-path
P\colon\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to31.25pt{\vbox to11.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.62642pt\lower-6.36386pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

,where,
\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathtt{r}^{\prime\prime}
conflict with
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
.
Note that
\mathtt{r}^{\prime\prime}\neq\mathtt{r}
, otherwise
\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime}=\mathtt{w}
since
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, implying that
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
is safe.
Since
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
is unsafe, we have
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\not\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})^{+}
and hence,
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime},\mathtt{w})\not\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})^{+}
.
Thus
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime})
is also unsafe, while clearly
\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime}\not\in\operatorname{InactiveWr}
.
∎

The completeness can now be established in three lemmas, one for each of the properties (1), (2i), and (2ii) above
(i.e.,
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
-acyclicity and minimal coherence).
Before we proceed, we will use the following notation to make our analysis easier.

Notation.

Given an event
e
, we say that
e
appears in phase
i
, written
\operatorname{phase}(e)=i
, if it writes/reads a value superscripted by
i
(i.e., of the form
v^{i}
or
\overline{v}^{i}
).
Similarly, we say
that
e
appears in step
j
, written
\operatorname{step}(e)=j
, if it writes/reads a value subscripted by
j
(i.e., of the form
v_{j}
or
\overline{v}_{j}
).
We define a quasi order
\preceq
on the event set
\mathsf{E}
with
e_{1}\preceq e_{2}
if either
(i)
\operatorname{phase}(e_{1})<\operatorname{phase}(e_{2})
or
(ii)
\operatorname{phase}(e_{1})=\operatorname{phase}(e_{2})
and
\operatorname{step}(e_{1})\leq\operatorname{step}(e_{2})
.
We also write
e_{1}\prec e_{2}
to denote that
e_{1}\preceq e_{2}
and
e_{2}\not\preceq e_{1}
.
Now consider an arbitrary path
P=e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
.
We say that
P

crosses
a thread
t
if
\mathsf{tid}(e_{\ell})
for some
for some
\ell\in[k]
.
We call
P

monotonic
if it linearizes
\preceq
, i.e.,
e_{\ell}\preceq e_{\ell+1}
for all
\ell\in[k-1]
.

We first establish the safety of each triplet.

Lemma 3.6.

Every triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
is safe.

Proof.

First, observe that the following hold by construction.

(1)

There is no memory location that is both written and read by the same thread.

(2)

For every two conflicting writes
\mathtt{w}_{1}
,
\mathtt{w}_{2}
, if there exist two reads
\mathtt{r}_{1}
,
\mathtt{r}_{2}
such that
(\mathtt{w}_{i},\mathtt{r}_{i})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
for each
i\in[2]
and
(\mathtt{r}_{1},\mathtt{r}_{2})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
, then
(\mathtt{w}_{2},\mathtt{w}_{1})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
.

Now, let
x
be the location accessed by a triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
, and assume there exists a
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x})
-path
P\colon\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to31.25pt{\vbox to11.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.62642pt\lower-6.36386pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
