.
If
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime},\mathtt{w})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}^{?}_{x}
, we are done.
Otherwise, due to
(2)
, we can’t have that
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}
.
Hence,
\mathtt{w}
and
\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime}
are in different threads, and by the construction of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x}
, we have
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime},\mathtt{w})\in\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x}
, implying that
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})^{+}
.
Hence the triplet is safe, as desired.
∎

Next, we establish the second ingredient of completeness, i.e., the acyclicity of
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
.

Lemma 3.7.

(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
is acyclic.

Proof.

First, note that there is no
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
-cycle crossing any of
g_{2}
and
g_{5}
,
as these threads only contain writes, and hence there is no way to enter them by an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edge.
Moreover, by construction, any
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
-path
P
that starts from a thread other than
g_{2}
and
g_{5}
is necessarily monotonic.

Indeed, all
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edges connect events of the same phase and step, while the only
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-edges
e_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}}e_{2}
with
\operatorname{phase}(e_{1})<\operatorname{phase}(e_{2})
appear in
g_{2}
and
g_{5}
.
Finally, the only
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-edges
e_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}}e_{2}
with
\operatorname{phase}(e_{1})=\operatorname{phase}(e_{2})
and
\operatorname{step}(e_{1})>\operatorname{step}(e_{2})
occur in threads
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
,
h_{3}
,
p
and
q
(see
Fig.

6
and
Fig.

7
).
However, in light of
Lemma

4.5
, these edges can be ignored in our analysis.

Thus, any potential
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
-cycle has to traverse events of the same phase and step.
A straightforward analysis of each instantiation of each gadget
(see
Figs.

4(b)
and
4(c)
,

Figs.

5(a)
and
5(b)
,

Figs.

6(b)
,
6(c)
and
6(d)
,

Figs.

7(b)
,
7(c)
and
7(d)
), and their combinations on common events, establishes that no
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
-cycle exists, as desired.
∎

Finally, we establish the acyclicity of
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})
.

Lemma 3.8.

(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})
is acyclic for all locations
x
.

Proof.

Observe that no memory location is both read and written by the same thread in any of the gadgets.
Hence, any
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})
-cycle
C
following an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edge
\mathtt{w}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}}\mathtt{r}
enters a thread that it cannot exit.
This means that
C
can only contain events of the same thread, which is forbidden by
Lemma

3.7
.
Thus we only need to reason about the absence of
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})
cycles.

Observe that for every memory location
x
except
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0.2578,0.6641}z_{2}}
, every
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})
-path is monotonic.
Hence any potential
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{x})
-cycle must traverse paths of the same phase and step, and thus be contained in one of the gadgets.
The absence of such cycles can be easily established by looking at the instantiations of these gadgets (
Figs.

4
,
5
,
6
and
7
).

On the other hand, consider the case
x={\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{1}}
(the analysis is similar for
x={\color[rgb]{0.4961,0.2578,0.6641}z_{2}}
).
Let
C
be a shortest
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{1}}}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{1}}})
-cycle.
If
C
contains only events of the same phase and step, it can be dismissed again by looking at the gadget in
Fig.

5
.
Otherwise,
C
is non-monotonic and must thus traverse the non-monotonic edge
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(g_{2},z_{1},u^{i+1}_{j})}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(g_{2},z_{1},\overline{u}^{i}_{j})}
.
From there it can continue to to threads
g_{1}
and thread
t_{5}
, following events
e
with
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(g_{2},z_{1},\overline{u}^{i}_{j})}\preceq e
.
It can be easily verified on
Fig.

5
that the next time
C
crosses
g_{2}
, it is on an event
e^{\prime}
with
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(g_{2},z_{1},u^{i+1}_{j})},e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{1}}}
, which contradicts the fact that
C
is a shortest cycle.
The desired result follows.
∎

Lemma

3.6
,
Lemma

3.7
and
Lemma

3.8
imply the completeness of the reduction.

Corollary 3.9.

If
\varphi
is satisfiable then
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
.

4.
Hardness for WRA, RA and SRA

In this section we prove
Theorem

1.1
for the range of models
\mathsf{SRA}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{M}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{WRA}
,
which also implies hardness specifically for
\mathsf{SRA}
,
\mathsf{RA}
, and
\mathsf{WRA}
.
Similarly to
Section

3
, our reduction uses unbounded values.
Again, we will make the final step towards
Theorem

1.1
in
Section

6
, which consists of a simple modification of the technique presented here.

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}

The copy gadget
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
(
8(a)
) captures the Boolean assignment to variable
s_{j}
in phase
i
.
There are two ways to realize this gadget, by choosing which of the two writes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
the read
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
observes.
Choosing the write of
t_{1}
(
8(b)
) corresponds to setting
s_{j}=\perp
and also forces
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
to read from
t_{4}
.
Choosing the write of
t_{2}
(
8(c)
) corresponds to setting
s_{j}=\top
and also forces
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
to read from
t_{5}
.
This
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
coupling is formalized in

.

).

4.1.
Reduction

Given a monotone formula
\varphi=\{\mathcal{C}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
over
n
variables
\{s_{j}\}_{j\in[n]}
, we construct an abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po})
with
\kappa=23
threads and accessing
d=26
memory locations such that
\varphi
is satisfiable iff
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathcal{M}
, for any memory model
\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathsf{SRA}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{M}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{WRA}
.

\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
follows the general scheme of
Fig.

3
.
Again, in phase
i
and step
j
we have a read event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
that can read from either of two writes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
(corresponding to
s_{j}=\perp
) or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
(corresponding to
s_{j}=\top
).
We use the same types of gadgets as in the previous section to force the desirable interaction patterns between threads.
However, the contents of each gadget are different to account for the different memory models.
In particular, these gadgets now rely on the weak-read-coherence axiom to couple the readers in threads
t_{3}
and
t_{6}
and force the 1-in-3 property in each clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, as opposed to the porf-acyclicity and relaxed-read-coherence axioms used in
Section

3
for
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
.

The copy gadget
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
.

The copy gadget
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
(
Fig.

8
) guarantees that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{1}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{4}
.
Besides
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}x_{2}}
, this gadget also uses locations
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0.2578,0.6641}y_{2}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9375,0.5938,0.1758}y_{3}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}y_{4}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.2969,0.1914,0.6211}y_{5}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9258,0.0625,0.1836}y_{6}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5273,0.8633,0.2383}y_{7}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}y_{8}}
.
Moreover, the gadget also contains two
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-edges out of
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
.
Though not shown explicitly, these
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-edges can be easily enforced by an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edge
\mathtt{w}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}}\mathtt{r}
, where
\mathtt{w}
and
\mathtt{r}
access a new memory location.
These events are independent to our analysis later, and will thus be ignored, i.e., we will only be considering the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-edges as they appear in the gadget.

The copy-down gadget
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
.

The copy-down gadget
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
(
Fig.

9
), as before, has a similar structure to the copy gadget
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
and guarantees that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{1}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{4}
.
Together, the two copy gadgets
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
and
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}

ensure that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{1}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})}
reads from thread
t_{1}
, guaranteeing a valid truth assignment on
s_{j}
.
Besides
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}x_{2}}
, this gadget also uses locations
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0.2578,0.6641}z_{2}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9375,0.5938,0.1758}z_{3}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}z_{4}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.2969,0.1914,0.6211}z_{5}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9258,0.0625,0.1836}z_{6}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5273,0.8633,0.2383}z_{7}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}z_{8}}
.
Moreover, the gadget also contains two
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-edges out of
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
, which, as argued above, will be ignored in our analysis.

The at-most-one-true gadgets
\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i}_{j,k}
.

For each
c\in[3]
, the at-most-one-true gadget (
Fig.

10
) guarantees that for every clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, the
c
-th pair of literals
(s_{j},s_{k})
in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is such that
at most one of
s_{j}
and
s_{k}
is set to true.
For each
c\in[3]
, the corresponding gadget contains one additional memory location
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}
.

The at-least-one-true gadget
\operatorname{ALOne}^{i}_{j,k,\ell}
.

The at-least-one-true gadget (
Fig.

11
) guarantees that for every clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
with three literals
s_{j},s_{k},s_{\ell}
, at least one of them is set to true.
It contains one additional memory location
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
.

Putting the gadgets together.

We obtain the abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
by appropriately connecting all gadgets and specifying the interleaving of events in common threads.

First, we serially connect all gadgets in their common threads by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
.
In particular,
\operatorname{Copy}^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}
appears before
\operatorname{Copy}^{i_{2}}_{j_{2}}
if
i_{1}<i_{2}
or
i_{1}=i_{2}
and
j_{1}<j_{2}
;

\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i_{1}}_{j_{1}}
appears before
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i_{2}}_{j_{2}}
if
i_{1}<i_{2}
or
i_{1}=i_{2}
and
j_{1}<j_{2}
;

\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i_{1}}_{j_{1},k_{1}}
appears before
\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i_{2}}_{j_{2},k_{2}}
if
i_{1}<i_{2}
, and finally

\operatorname{ALOne}^{i_{1}}_{j_{1},k_{1},\ell_{1}}
appears before
\operatorname{ALOne}^{i_{2}}_{j_{2},k_{2},\ell_{2}}
if
i_{1}<i_{2}
.
Second, observe that the threads
t_{i}
, for
i\in[6]
, appear in multiple gadgets, thus we have to specify how to interleave their corresponding events.
We do so by first fixing a total order on memory locations

\sigma={\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0.2578,0.6641}y_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.9375,0.5938,0.1758}y_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}y_{4}},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0.2578,0.6641}z_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.9375,0.5938,0.1758}z_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}z_{4}},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
.

(1)

The read events succeeding every
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
in each gadget (i.e., those on locations
\{{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{\ell}},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in[4]}
,
\{{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}\}_{c\in[3]}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
) are
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-ordered according to
\sigma

(note that reads on some locations, such as
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
, might not appear at all after
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
, e.g., if clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
does not contain variable
s_{j}
).
Moreover, all these reads appear before any read on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}
that is a
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-successor of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
(in particular, reads on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}
with phase
\geq i
or reads with phase
i
and step
\geq j
).

(2)

Similarly, the write events preceding every
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
in each gadget are
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-ordered according to
\sigma
.
Moreover, all these writes appear after any write on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}}
that is a
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-predecessor of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
.
Likewise for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{4},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{5},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
.

Finally, observe that we have indeed used
\kappa=23
threads and
d=26
memory locations.
For the latter, we also count one extra memory location for each
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
edge out of thread
t_{6}
, thus
4
in total.

4.2.
Soundness

We start with the soundness of the reduction, in particular, if
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{WRA}
(and thus also
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathcal{M}
) then
\varphi
is satisfiable.
We achieve this by proving a sequence of intermediate lemmas similar to
Section

3
, however, each lemma now requires reasoning about the semantics of
\mathsf{WRA}
.
Recall that we assign
s_{j}=\top
if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
for all
i\in[m]
, and
s_{j}=\perp
if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
for all
i\in[m]
.
The first lemma is based on the copy gadgets
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
and
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
and states that each phase of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
makes consistent choices for the writer of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
(i.e., whether it reads from
t_{1}
or
t_{2}
), which makes the above assignment well-defined.
It follows from the high-level description of these two gadgets and the accompanying
Fig.

8
and
Fig.

9
.

Lemma 4.1.

Let
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})
be a concrete extension of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
that satisfies weak-read-coherence.
For all
i_{1},i_{2}\in[m]
,
j\in[n]
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i_{1}}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i_{1}}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
iff
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i_{2}}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i_{2}}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.

The next lemma is based on the at-most-one-true gadgets
\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i}_{j,k}
and states that for every clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and for each of the
c\in[3]
pair of variables
(s_{j},s_{k})
in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, at most one of them is assigned to true.
Again, it follows by a direct analysis on the accompanying
Fig.

10
.

Lemma 4.2.

Let
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})
be a concrete extension of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
that satisfies weak-read-coherence.
For every
i\in[m]
and
j,k\in[n]

such that
s_{j}
and
s_{k}
appear in clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
or
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.

The third lemma is based on the at-least-one-true gadget
\operatorname{ALOne}^{i}_{j,k,\ell}
, and it is used to show that for every clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}=(s_{j},s_{k},s_{\ell})
, at least one of its variables is assigned to true.
Again, it follows by a direct analysis on the accompanying
Fig.

11
.

Lemma 4.3.

Let
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})
be a concrete extension of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
that satisfies weak-read-coherence.
For every
i\in[m]
and
j,k,\ell\in[n]
such that
s_{j}
,
s_{k}
and
s_{\ell}
appear in clause
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
or
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
or
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.

Lemma

4.1
states that our truth assignment for
\varphi
is valid, while
Lemma

4.2
and
Lemma

4.3
guarantee that in every clause, exactly one literal is set to true.
Hence we have the following corollary.

Corollary 4.4.

If
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{WRA}
then
\varphi
is satisfiable.

4.3.
Completeness

We now turn our attention to the completeness property, i.e., if
\varphi
is satisfiable then
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{SRA}
(and thus also
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathcal{M}
).
We use the notions of phase, step, and monotonicity as in
Section

3
.
We construct a reads-from relation
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and a partial modification order
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
as follows.

(1)

For each gadget, we insert
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}
edges according to
Figs.

8
,
9
,
10
and
11
and the truth assignments on variables
s_{j}
,
s_{k}
,
s_{\ell}
involved in that gadget.
In particular, this implies that, for each
i\in[m]
and
j\in[n]
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
if
s_{j}=\perp
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
if
s_{j}=\top
.
Observe that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
is fully specified, i.e., every read has a write to read from.
Note that for every pair
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
we have
\operatorname{phase}(\mathtt{w})=\operatorname{phase}(\mathtt{r})
and
\operatorname{step}(\mathtt{w})=\operatorname{step}(\mathtt{r})
, thus
\mathtt{r}\preceq\mathtt{w}
.

(2)

For every two conflicting writes
\mathtt{w}_{1},\mathtt{w}_{2}
such that
(i)
\mathtt{w}_{1}\prec\mathtt{w}_{2}
, and
(ii)
(\mathtt{w}_{1},\mathtt{w}_{2})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
,
we have
(\mathtt{w}_{1},\mathtt{w}_{2})\in\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
.
Thus, for any two conflicting writes
\mathtt{w}_{1},\mathtt{w}_{2}
with
\mathtt{w}_{1}\prec\mathtt{w}_{2}
we have
(\mathtt{w}_{1},\mathtt{w}_{2})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})^{+}
.

We call a triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})

safe
if either
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
or
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})^{+}
.
To prove the consistency of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
, it suffices to argue that
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
is
minimally coherent
,
namely, that
(i)
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})^{+}
is acyclic, and
(ii) every triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
is safe.
Indeed, these two conditions guarantee that
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
can be linearized to a total
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}
such that any extension
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
satisfies
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})\models\mathsf{SRA}

(
Tunç et al. 2023
)
,
which implies that also
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\models\mathcal{M}
.

In order to simplify our analysis, we again ignore the writes in threads
\{h_{c}\}_{c\in[3]}
,
p
, and
q
that are not read in
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
(crossed-out in

Fig.

10
and
Fig.

11
).
This allows us to make our formal statements more uniform, while it does not affect the correctness of the analysis.
Indeed, let

\operatorname{InactiveWr}=\{\mathtt{w}\in\mathsf{W}\colon\mathsf{tid}(\mathtt{w})\in\{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},p,q\}\text{ and }\not\exists\mathtt{r}\in\mathsf{R}.~(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}\}
.
The following lemma is straightforward.

Lemma 4.5.

The following statements hold.

(1)

If there is an
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})
-cycle then there is such a cycle without any write in
\operatorname{InactiveWr}
.

(2)

If there is an unsafe triplet, then there is such a triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
where
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\not\in\operatorname{InactiveWr}
.

We start with condition (i) of minimal coherence, i.e., we need to show that
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})
is acyclic.
Observe that each individual gadget is free from
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})
-cycles, regardless of how we resolve the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
edges associated with it (see
Figs.

8(b)
and
8(c)
,

Figs.

9(a)
and
9(b)
,

Figs.

10(b)
,
10(c)
and
10(d)
,
Figs.

11(b)
,
11(c)
and
11(d)
).
However, we have to also argue that the interleaving of these gadgets is free from
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})
-cycles.

Our first key lemma states that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
paths between writes are, without loss of generality, monotonic.
This is based on three observations.
First, due to
Lemma

4.5
, we can ignore writes in the threads
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
,
h_{3}
,
p
and
q
, which contain
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-edges that would violate this statement.
Second, all
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edges connect events of the same phase and step.
Third, the only
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-edges that are non-monotonic enter read events (in particular, a read
{\color[rgb]{0.9258,0.0625,0.1836}\mathtt{r}(v^{i}_{j},z_{6},g_{1})}
or a read
{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}\mathtt{r}(v^{i}_{j},z_{8},g_{4})}
in
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
).
Since the only possible continuation of an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-path out of a read event is to take another
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-edge, we can remove the first non-monotonic edge (as
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
is transitive) and obtain a new valid
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-path.
Repeating this process results in a monotonic
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-path between the writes.
Formally, we have the following.

Lemma 4.6.

For every two writes
\mathtt{w}_{1}
,
\mathtt{w}_{2}
, if
(\mathtt{w}_{1},\mathtt{w}_{2})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
then there exists a monotonic
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-path
\mathtt{w}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox to12.34pt{\vbox to10.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.16943pt\lower-5.6833pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

.

We can now prove the acyclicity condition of minimal coherence.
Intuitively, any potential
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})
-cycle
C
can be seen as a sequence of write events connected by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
and
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
.
By construction, every edge
\mathtt{w}_{1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}}\mathtt{w}_{2}
is monotonic,
while, due to
Lemma

4.6
, every subpath
\mathtt{w}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox to12.34pt{\vbox to10.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.16943pt\lower-5.6833pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

of
C
is, without loss of generality, monotonic.
Thus
C
is monotonic, and since it is a cycle, every event in
C
has the same phase and step.
The absence of such cycles can then be directly established by inspecting the gadgets in
Figs.

8
,
9
,
10
and
11
.

Lemma 4.7.

(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})
is acyclic.

Next, we turn our attention to the second condition of minimal coherence, i.e., we argue that every triplet is safe.
We first prove a general statement that prohibits
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-paths to a read
\mathtt{r}
from writes
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
that are
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-successors to the write
\mathtt{w}
that
\mathtt{r}
reads from.
This will help us establish the safety of each triplet, and will also prove useful later in
Section

5.1
when we address Causal Memory.

Lemma 4.8.

For every pair
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and write
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
with
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
, we have
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
.

To realize
Lemma

4.8
, we first argue that any path
P\colon\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to12.34pt{\vbox to10.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.16943pt\lower-5.6833pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.61667pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.49 -6.62 L 2.73 -6.62 L 1.54 -7.59 L -0.82 -5.65 L -3.18 -7.59 L -5.54 -5.65 L -8.26 -6.62 L -8.26 -6.62}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.96947pt}{-4.78331pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathtt{r}
contains events of the same phase and step.
Indeed, as no location is ever written and read by the same thread,
P
has the general form

On the other hand, we have

.

, and since

,

,

and

.

can then be established by a careful inspection of these gadgets.

Again, a careful inspection of
Figs.

8
,
9
,
10
and
11
and the use of
Lemma

4.8
show that
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
is indeed safe.

Lemma 4.9.

Every triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
is safe.

Lemma

4.7
and
Lemma

4.9
show that
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
is indeed minimally coherent, which implies that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})\models\mathsf{SRA}
.
Thus we have the following corollary.

Corollary 4.10.

If
\varphi
is satisfiable then
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{SRA}
.

Together,
Corollary

4.4
and
Corollary

4.10
establish the correctness of the reduction, i.e.,
\varphi
is satisfiable iff
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathcal{M}
for any memory model
\mathsf{SRA}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{M}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{WRA}
.

5.
Implications and Other Memory Models

Our proof of
Theorem

1.1
is strong enough to yield hardness on other popular memory models across different domains.
In this section, we explore its implications.

Causal Consistency models.

In a distributed setting, consistency commonly captures the concept of
causality
.
Three of the most standard causal models are the basic Causal Consistency (
\mathsf{CC}
), Causal Convergence (
\mathsf{CCv}
), and Causal Memory (
\mathsf{CM}
)
(
Bouajjani et al. 2017
)
.
It was recently shown that
\mathsf{CC}
coincides with
\mathsf{WRA}
while
\mathsf{CCv}
coincides with
\mathsf{SRA}

(
Lahav and Boker 2022
)
.
Thus,
Theorem

1.1
implies
NP
-completeness for all models between
\mathsf{CCv}
and
\mathsf{CC}
.
In
Section

5.1
we also establish
NP
-completeness for
\mathsf{CM}
, by extending the proof of
Theorem

1.1
, thereby completing
Theorem

1.2
.

Hardware memory models.

Next, we turn our attention to some popular hardware memory models, namely, for the POWER and x86-TSO architectures, as well as PSO.
We show that
Theorem

1.1
implies
NP
-completeness for POWER,
but consistency checks for TSO/PSO run in polynomial time.

The fully Relaxed model.

Finally, we consider the Relaxed model which does not require
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf})
-acyclicity,
and remark that this brings the problem in polynomial time.
Interestingly,
\mathsf{Relaxed}
is the only non-multi-copy atomic model in our list for which consistency testing is tractable.

5.1.
Implications for Causal Memory

Here we prove the case (ii) of
Theorem

1.2
, i.e., the hardness of bounded consistency testing for any memory model
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathsf{CC}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{M}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{CM}
.
Instead of performing a separate reduction, we reuse our reduction from
Section

4
.
Let
\varphi=\{\mathcal{C}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
be a Boolean formula over
n
variables
\{s_{j}\}_{j\in[n]}
and
m
clauses of the form
\mathcal{C}_{i}=(s_{j},s_{k},s_{\ell})
, for which we have to solve Monotone 3SAT.
Moreover, let
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po})
be the abstract execution as constructed in
Section

4
.
Since
\mathsf{WRA}
coincides with
\mathsf{CC}

(
Lahav and Boker 2022
)
, we have the following soundness corollary.

Corollary 5.1.

If
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{CC}
then
\varphi
is satisfiable.

Thus, to complete the theorem, it remains to show that if
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{CM}
then
\varphi
is satisfiable.
Towards this, we next formalize
\mathsf{CM}
and then prove the statement.

Causal Memory.

Causal memory is similar to Causal Consistency but further requires that each thread has a locally-consistent view of the order which different writes have been executed
(
Ahamad et al. 1995
;
Bouajjani et al. 2017
)
.
This is made formal by introducing one additional relation for each thread, called the
observed-before
relation
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}}
.

The observed-before relation
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}}
.

Given an event
e
, the
observed-before relation
‡
‡

‡

Other works refer to this relation as “happened-before” for
e
. We avoid this term here to not confuse it with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
.
for
e
is the smallest transitive relation
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}\subseteq\mathsf{E}\times\mathsf{E}
with the following properties.

(1)

For every
(e_{1},e_{2})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
such that
(e_{i},e)\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
for each
i\in[2]
, we have
(e_{1},e_{2})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
.

(2)

For every conflicting triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
such that
(i)
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
and
(ii)
(\mathtt{r},e)\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}^{?}
,
we have
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
.

Intuitively, when
t
executed
\mathtt{r}
, it must have observed
\mathtt{w}
after
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
, so that
\mathtt{r}
indeed obtained its value from
\mathtt{w}
.
The
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
relation specifies that this ordering cannot change later when
t
executes
e
.
Notice the fixpoint style of the above definition.
As we order
(\mathtt{w}_{1},\mathtt{w}_{2})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
(and since the relation is transitive and contains
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
),
more and more write-read pairs satisfy property (i)
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
, triggering the addition of new orderings
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
.
For any two events
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
with
(e_{1},e_{2})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
, we have
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e_{1}}}\subseteq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e_{2}}}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}}
grows monotonically as we go downwards in each thread.
The observed-before relation for a thread
t
is defined as
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t}}=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e^{\max}}}
, where
e^{\max}
is the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-maximal event of
t
.
The new axiom requires that
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t}}
is irreflexive
(
Bouajjani et al. 2017
)
.

•

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t}}
is irreflexive for each thread
t
(ob-acyclicity)

In turn,
\mathsf{CM}
is equal to
\mathsf{WRA}
with ob-acyclicity as an extra axiom.

•

(\text{porf-acyclicity{}})\land(\text{weak-read-coherence{}})\land(\text{ob-acyclicity{}})
[
\mathsf{CM}
]

t_{1}
\mathtt{w}(x)
\mathtt{r}(x)
t_{2}
\mathtt{w}(x)
\mathtt{r}(x)
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t_{1}}}
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t_{2}}}

(a)

\mathsf{CM}
-consistent

t_{1}
\mathtt{w}(z)
\mathtt{w}(z)
\mathtt{w}(x)
\mathtt{w}(y)
t_{2}
\mathtt{w}(x)
\mathtt{r}(z)
\mathtt{r}(y)
\mathtt{r}(x)
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t_{2}}}
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t_{2}}}

(b)

\mathsf{CM}
-inconsistent

t_{1}
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}(x)
t_{2}
\mathtt{w}(x)
t_{3}
\mathtt{r}(x)
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t_{3}}}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}

(c)

5.3

(

\mathsf{CM}
, as each of

and

is acyclic, but inconsistent in
\mathsf{SRA}
.
(

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t_{2}}}
creates a cycle, but consistent in
\mathsf{SRA}
.
(

) Illustration of the argument behind

.
Since
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}(x)
reaches an earlier event in
t_{3}
than
\mathtt{w}(x)
, the edge
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}(x)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t_{3}}}}\mathtt{w}(x)
does not create a new path from
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}(x)
to

.

Observe that
\mathsf{WRA}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{CM}
, but
\mathsf{CM}
is incomparable with
\mathsf{RA}
/
\mathsf{SRA}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{CM}
allows executions that are inconsistent in
\mathsf{RA}
/
\mathsf{SRA}
and vice versa.
See

and

for illustrations.

Next, we prove the completeness of the construction, i.e., if
\varphi
is satisfiable then
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{CM}
.
Consider the reads-from relation
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and the partial modification order
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
exactly as constructed in the completeness argument of
Section

4
(i.e., following the gadgets in
Figs.

8
,
9
,
10
and
11
).
Let
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})
be the execution witnessing the
\mathsf{SRA}
-consistency of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
according to
Corollary

4.10
.
Since
\mathsf{SRA}
satisfies the porf-acyclicity and weak-read-coherence axioms, we only need to argue that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
also satisfies ob-acyclicity to conclude that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\models\mathsf{CM}
.
For this, we have to establish some additional lemmas.

Our first lemma stems from
Lemma

4.8
and states an important property of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
: for every pair
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
,
\mathtt{w}
has no
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-path to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-predecessors of
\mathtt{r}
.
In other words, the first event of the thread of
\mathtt{r}
that
\mathtt{w}
can reach by means of an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-path is
\mathtt{r}
itself via the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edge
\mathtt{w}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}}\mathtt{r}
.

Lemma 5.2.

For every
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and event
e
such that
(e,\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
, we have
(\mathtt{w},e)\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
.

Next, we define a “one-hop” variant of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}}
.
Given an event
e
, the
one-hop observed-before relation
for
e
is the smallest transitive relation
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{e}}\subseteq\mathsf{E}\times\mathsf{E}
with the following properties.

(1)

For every
(e_{1},e_{2})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
such that
(e_{i},e)\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
for each
i\in[2]
, we have
(e_{1},e_{2})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{e}}
.

(2)

For every event
e
and conflicting triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
such that
(i)
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
and
(ii)
(\mathtt{r},e)\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}^{?}
,
we have
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{e}}
.

Contrasting
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{e}}
to
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
, the only difference is in condition (2i):
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
checks whether
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
, while
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{e}}
checks the weaker condition
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
.
Thus
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{e}}
does not have the fixpoint style of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
.
Similarly to
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t}}
, we let
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{t}}=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{e^{\max}}}
, where
e^{\max}
is the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-maximal event of thread
t
.

Our next lemma states that for our execution
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
coincides with
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{e}}
.
In other words,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
reaches a fixpoint after only one iteration.
This observation stems from
Lemma

5.2
.
Intuitively, since for each triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
,
\mathtt{w}
cannot
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-reach any
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-predecessor of
\mathtt{r}
,
traversing an edge
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}}\mathtt{w}
cannot lead to any events of the thread of
\mathtt{r}
that weren’t already reachable via the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-path
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to12.34pt{\vbox to10.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.16943pt\lower-5.6833pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

that made us insert
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
in the first place (see
Fig.

12(c)
).
Hence, adding such an ordering
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
cannot lead to further firings of condition (2i) of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{e}}
.
Formally, we have the following.

Lemma 5.3.

For every thread
t
, we have
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{t}}=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t}}
.

Finally, observe that whenever we add
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{e}}
, we have
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
.
Due to
Lemma

4.9
, the triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
is safe, thus
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})^{+}
.
Hence, the acyclicity of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}_{t}}=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}ob}^{1}_{t}}
follows from the acyclicity of
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})^{+}
(
Lemma

4.7
).
We thus have the following lemma, which, together with
Corollary

5.1
, completes the proof case (ii) of
Theorem

1.2
.

Lemma 5.4.

If
\varphi
is satisfiable, then
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{CM}
.

5.2.
Implications for POWER

The memory model of the
\mathsf{POWER}
architecture is defined on load, store, atomic read-modify-write memory accesses, and various types of fences.

\mathsf{POWER}
orders memory accesses based on fences, address, data, and control dependencies, while, again, coherence forces a total order on same-location accesses.
In addition,
\mathsf{POWER}
defines two global orderings, namely,
happens-before
, and
propagation
.
The happens-before relation is based on dependencies, fences, and the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
relation across threads.
The propagation relation captures the propagation of read and written values by combining fences, happens-before,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}
.
Based on these relations
\mathsf{POWER}
defines its consistency axioms, which we will not present here; instead, we refer the interested readers to
(
Alglave et al. 2014
)
.

Lahav et al. 2016
showed that
\mathsf{SRA}
captures precisely the guarantees of
\mathsf{POWER}
for programs that are compiled from the release-acquire fragment of C/C++.
In turn, this implies that the result established in
Section

4
for
\mathsf{SRA}
transfers over to
\mathsf{POWER}
.
We thus have the following corollary.

See
1.3

5.3.
What About x86-TSO and PSO?

Our results so far prove strong hardness for testing a variety of weak-memory models.
In contrast, in this section, we outline that the problem is solvable in polynomial time for x86-TSO and PSO.
Conceptually, this is less surprising for
\mathsf{TSO}
, which diverges only a little from
\mathsf{SC}
, but is more so for
\mathsf{PSO}
, which allows for behaviors that are not even causally consistent.

t_{1}
\mathtt{w}(x)
\mathtt{w}(x)
\mathtt{r}(y)
t_{2}
\mathtt{w}(y)
\mathtt{w}(y)
\mathtt{r}(x)
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}

(a)

\mathsf{TSO}
-consistent

t_{1}
\mathtt{w}(x)
\mathtt{w}(x)
\mathtt{w}(y)
t_{2}
\mathtt{r}(y)
\mathtt{r}(x)
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}

(b)

\mathsf{PSO}
-consistent

t_{1}
\mathtt{w}(x)
\mathtt{r}(x)
t_{2}
\mathtt{w}(x)
\mathtt{r}(x)
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}

(c)

\mathsf{TSO}
/
\mathsf{PSO}
-inconsistent

Figure 13
.

(
13(a)
) An execution consistent in
\mathsf{TSO}
, as well as in
\mathsf{PSO}
and all other memory models we have considered, but not
\mathsf{SC}
.
(
13(b)
) An execution consistent in
\mathsf{PSO}
, as well as in
\mathsf{Relaxed\text{-}Acyclic}
but not in
\mathsf{TSO}
or even
\mathsf{WRA}
.
(
13(c)
) An execution inconsistent in
\mathsf{TSO}
/
\mathsf{PSO}
, but consistent in
\mathsf{CC}
/
\mathsf{WRA}
.

Total Store Order.

The
\mathsf{TSO}
model deviates from
\mathsf{SC}
by introducing a write-buffer for each thread, which acts as a FIFO queue
(
Sewell et al. 2010
)
.
When a thread
t
executes a write
\mathtt{w}(t,x)
, this does not modify the shared memory immediately and is thus not visible to the other threads.
Instead,
\mathtt{w}(t,x)
is stored in the buffer of
t
.
The buffer non-deterministically flushes some of its writes to the shared memory, at which point they become visible to the other threads.
On the other hand, when a thread
t
executes a read
\mathtt{r}(t,x)
, it is forced to read from the most recent write
\mathtt{w}(t,x)
in
t
’s buffer.
If no such write exists then
\mathtt{r}(t,x)
reads from the shared memory.
See
Fig.

13(a)
for an illustration.

For capturing the complexity of consistency-testing of an abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po})
under
\mathsf{TSO}
, it is helpful to switch to operational semantics.
The semantics are defined by means of a labeled transition system
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
; as this is a standard model, we defer the description of the formal model to
Section

B.2
.
In high level, a state in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
is a triplet
\langle\operatorname{P},\operatorname{B},\operatorname{M}\rangle
, where

(1)

\operatorname{P}\subseteq\mathsf{E}
is the set of events that have been executed so far.

(2)

\operatorname{B}\colon\mathcal{T}\to(\mathsf{W})^{*}
maps every thread
t
to a sequence of writes
\mathtt{w}(t,x_{1}),\mathtt{w}(t,x_{2}),\dots,\mathtt{w}(t,x_{i})
, which represents the state of the buffer of thread
t
.

(3)

\operatorname{M}\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathsf{W}
maps every memory location of the shared memory to the most recent write to it.

A counting argument shows that the size of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
is bounded by
\kappa^{d}\cdot n^{O(\kappa^{2})}
, for
n=|\mathsf{E}|
,
\kappa
threads,
d
locations, and thus becomes polynomial when
\kappa,d=O(1)
.
We refer to
Section

B.2
for details.

Partial Store Order (PSO).

The
\mathsf{PSO}
model
(
SPARC International 1994
)
is similar to
\mathsf{TSO}
, with the difference that every thread has a different buffer for each location.
This allows both write-read reorderings (like
\mathsf{TSO}
) and write-write reorderings on different locations.
This induces more behaviors than
\mathsf{TSO}
, but is incomparable to some other models like
\mathsf{WRA}
(see
Fig.

13(b)
and
Fig.

13(c)
).
The operational semantics can be defined by means of an LTS
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{PSO}}
analogously to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
.
A similar analysis shows that the size of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{PSO}}
is bounded by
n^{O(\kappa(\kappa+d))}
.
Hence we have the following theorem, which differentiates
\mathsf{TSO}
/
\mathsf{PSO}
from the other weak-memory models we have seen so far.

See
1.4

5.4.
A Final Note on Relaxed

Finally, we turn our attention to the
\mathsf{Relaxed}
model.
The only two axioms of this model are relaxed-write-coherence and relaxed-read-coherence, which concern individual memory locations,
and guarantee per-location coherence, i.e.,
focusing on each location individually, the corresponding execution is
\mathsf{SC}
-consistent.
Given an abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
, to decide whether
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{Relaxed}
, it suffices to check whether
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{x}\models\mathsf{SC}
for each location
x
,
where
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{x}
occurs from
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
by considering only events accessing
x
.
As each consistency check
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{x}\models\mathsf{SC}
takes polynomial time
(
Agarwal et al. 2021
)
,
and we clearly have polynomially many such checks, we arrive at
Corollary

1.5
.

See
1.5

6.
Hardness with Bounded Values

For ease of presentation, our reductions in
Section

3
and
Section

4
use a bounded number of threads and memory locations but an unbounded value domain.
Indeed, given the Boolean formula
\varphi=\{\mathcal{C}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
on
m
clauses and
n
variables, the value domain of the abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
has size
\Theta(n\cdot m)
.
In this section we outline how to modify those reductions so that
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
also uses a bounded value domain, thereby arriving at
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
.

Intuition.

Our two reductions are such that every read
\mathtt{r}
can read from at most three writes, and these appear in the same gadget as
\mathtt{r}
.
However, the values of these events are specific to the gadget, and in particular,
specific to the phase
i
and the step
j
of the events (i.e., events are of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},t_{3},v^{i}_{j})}
).
Our strategy for decreasing the size of the value domain (of both reductions in
Section

4
and
Section

3
) is by using repeating values which are not parameterized by the superscript
i
and subscript
j

(i.e., the events in the executions constructed now look like
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(x_{1},t_{3},v)}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(x_{1},t_{1},v)}
).
This change does not affect completeness but threatens soundness, as now,
some read events may read from write events in other gadgets that were
previously forbidden simply because their values were not matching.
To avoid this, we slightly modify our abstract executions
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
by inserting a bounded number of auxiliary write and read events between consecutive gadgets, which also access a bounded number of values.
The auxiliary write events write dummy values read by the auxiliary read events.
The effect of these additional reads-from edges due to auxiliary events
is to create
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}_{x}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}_{x})^{+}
paths that once again forbid the
original (i.e., non-auxiliary) read events of a gadget to
access write events from other gadgets (while obeying the desired consistency axioms).

Construction.

We now outline the construction.
The process is similar for both the abstract executions of
Section

3
and
Section

4
.
For this reason, we describe it generically on an abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
.
Our transformation is carried out in two steps,
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\mathrel{\hbox to7.83pt{\vbox to5.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.9171pt\lower-3.25276pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.36433pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.38 -3.26 L -0.39 -3.26 L -1.57 -4.22 L -3.93 -2.29 L -5.14 -3.26 L -5.14 -3.26}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{1.71713pt}{-2.35277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{2}
, where
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{1}
and
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{2}
have the same number of threads and locations as
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
, and
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{2}
additionally has a bounded value domain.

Step 1.

We obtain
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{1}
by inserting various events in
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
while keeping the threads and memory locations the same.
We start by fixing a total order
\sigma_{1}
on locations, and a total order
\sigma_{2}
on threads.

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}x_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}y_{1}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}y_{8}},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}z_{1}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}z_{8}},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{1}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=t_{1},\dots,t_{6},f_{1},\dots,f_{6},g_{1},\dots,g_{6},h_{1},\dots,h_{3},p,q

Fix a phase
i
and step
j
and let
\zeta=(i*m+j)\mod 2
.
For a location
x
of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
, different from
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
,
we introduce auxiliary write and read events on
x
as follows:
(i) if a thread
t
writes on
x
,we insert a write
\mathtt{w}(t,x,v^{t}_{\zeta})
after all events of phase
i
and step
j
in
t
, and
(ii) if a thread
t
reads from
x
, we insert a sequence of read events
\mathtt{r}(t,x,v^{t^{1}}_{\zeta}),\mathtt{r}(t,x,v^{t^{2}}_{\zeta}),\dots
before all events of phase
i
and step
j+1
(or phase
i+1
and step
1
, if
j=n
), where
t^{1},t^{2},\dots
is the subsequence of
\sigma_{2}
of threads writing to
x
values read by thread
t
.
We repeat this process for all locations
x\not\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\}

in the order of appearance in the total order
\sigma_{1}
, placing the auxiliary writes before the auxiliary reads in each thread.
Observe that each
\mathtt{r}(t,x,v^{t^{\ell}}_{\zeta})
event is forced to read from the respective
\mathtt{w}(t^{\ell},x,v^{t^{\ell}}_{\zeta})
.

Next, we turn our attention to the locations
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{3}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
.
The auxiliary events are positioned similarly, except for the detail about the step number
j
,
because accesses to these locations span an entire phase (in the at-most-one-true and at-least-one-true gadgets).
In particular, we have
\zeta=i\mod 2
, while auxiliary write events are placed in each thread after all events of phase
i
, and read events are placed before events of phase
i+1
.

Observe that since the number of threads and locations is bounded in
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
, the same holds for
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{1}
, while the additional values accessed by the auxiliary events in
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{1}
are also bounded.

Step 2.

In the second step, we transform
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{1}
to
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{2}
so that the latter only accesses a bounded number of values.
In particular, we make
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{2}
identical to
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{1}
with the difference that, for every event of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{1}
that also appears in
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
(i.e., non-auxiliary events),
we remove from its value the superscript of the phase and the subscript of the step of that event.
For example, each write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
in
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{1}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v)}
in
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{2}
.
It is straightforward to verify that
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{2}
has a bounded domain of threads, locations, and values.
Moreover,
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}_{2}
is consistent in the respective memory model iff
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
is, by repeating the arguments in
Section

3
and
Section

4
, this time also accounting for the auxiliary events.

7.
Conclusion

We have studied the standard problem of consistency-testing for various popular weak-memory models spanning across software, hardware, and distributed systems.
We have shown that even the
bounded
version of consistency testing is
NP
-complete in most of these models, i.e., when every natural input parameter is bounded.
This is a significant improvement over an abundance of prior hardness results which primarily stemmed from parameters such as the number of threads or memory locations being unbounded.
Our results thus highlight the true intricacies of weak-memory testing.
In particular, our results imply that the problem provably admits no parameterization with respect to natural input parameters.
Interesting future work includes the possibility of extending our hardness to other memory models such as the one in ARM architectures,
as well as recovering tractability by imposing further restrictions (such as context/view-switching).

Proof.

We argue by induction that for every
i\in[m-1]
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
iff
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i+1}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
First, note that if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, then the copy gadget
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
forces
({\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{w}(t_{4},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Indeed, we have the following forced sequence of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
edges (see
Fig.

8(b)
).
We begin the sequence with the first edge
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, marked
1
in
Fig.

.

(1)

We have
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},y_{1},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
due to weak-read-coherence.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})})
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(f_{4},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.

(2)

, otherwise we would have
({\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}\mathtt{w}(f_{5},y_{4},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},y_{4},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}

{\color[rgb]{0.5273,0.8633,0.2383}\mathtt{r}(f_{4},y_{7},v^{i}_{j})}

({\color[rgb]{0.5273,0.8633,0.2383}\mathtt{w}(f_{6},y_{7},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.5273,0.8633,0.2383}\mathtt{r}(f_{4},y_{7},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.

Proof.

The statement follows by analyzing the at-most-one-true gadget
\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i}_{j,k}
, where
c\in[3]
is such that
(s_{j},s_{k})
is the
c
-th pair of variables in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
(see
Fig.

10
).

First, we argue that if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
then
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Indeed, we have the following forced sequence of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
edges (see
1
-
4
in
Fig.

10(b)

starting with
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
marked
1
).

(1)

We have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, and thus
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, otherwise we would have a violation of weak-read-coherence.
Hence
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
This is marked
2
in

.

(2)

We now have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, and thus
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, otherwise we would have a violation of weak-read-coherence.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
This is marked
3
in
Fig.

10(b)
.

(3)

In turn, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, as otherwise we would have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},\overline{v}^{i}_{2})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, which would violate weak-read coherence.
Hence we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, marked
4
in
Fig.

10(b)
.

Second, we argue that if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
then
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Indeed, we have the following forced sequence of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
edges (marked
1
-
4
, see
Fig.

10(c)
, starting with
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
marked

1
).

(1)

We have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},\overline{v}^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, and thus
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, otherwise we would have a violation of weak-read-coherence.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
This is marked
2
in
Fig.

10(c)
.

(2)

We have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, as otherwise we would have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
which would violate weak-read-coherence, given that

({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and

({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{\ell},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}\subseteq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, see
3
in
Fig.

10(c)
.

(3)

In turn, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, as otherwise we would have

({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, which would violate weak-read coherence.
Thus,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, depicted by
4
in
Fig.

10(c)
.

∎

See
4.3

Proof.

The statement follows by analyzing the at-least-one-true gadget
\operatorname{ALOne}^{i}_{j,k,\ell}
(see
Fig.

11
).
We assume wlog that
\mathcal{C}_{i}=(s_{j},s_{k},s_{\ell})
, i.e., the three variables appear in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
in this order.

First, we argue that if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
then
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Indeed, we have the following forced sequence of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
edges (see
1
-
4
,
Fig.

11(b)
. We begin with

({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}

both marked
1
).

(1)

We have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},b,\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, and thus
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, otherwise we would have a violation of weak-read-coherence.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{j})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(p,b,v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Similarly, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},b,\overline{v}^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, and thus
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, otherwise we would have a violation of weak-read-coherence.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{k})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(q,b,v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
These two edges are marked
2
.

(2)

We now have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(p,b,v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, and thus
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(p,b,v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, otherwise we would have a violation of weak-read coherence.
Similarly, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(q,b,v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, and thus
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(q,b,v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, otherwise we would have a violation of weak-read-coherence.
Thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{\ell})}
is forced to read from the only other available write, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},b,v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
This is marked as
3
.

(3)

In turn, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, as otherwise we would have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},b,\overline{v}^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
, which would violate weak-read-coherence.
Thus we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
, marked
4
.

A similar analysis establishes that if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
then
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
(see
Fig.

11(c)
), as well as that if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{k})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{\ell})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
then
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
(see
Fig.

11(d)
).
The desired result follows.
∎

See
4.4

See

Proof.

(1)

Any cycle containing a write

must contain a sequence of edges

, which can be replaced by the single edge

.

{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.49 -6.62 L 2.73 -6.62 L 1.54 -7.59 L -0.82 -5.65 L -3.18 -7.59 L -5.54 -5.65 L -8.26 -6.62 L -8.26 -6.62}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.96947pt}{-4.78331pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathtt{r}
, where,
\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathtt{r}^{\prime\prime}
conflict with
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
.
Note that
\mathtt{r}^{\prime\prime}\neq\mathtt{r}
, otherwise, by definition of a triplet, we will have
\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime}=\mathtt{w}
since
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
. This would
imply that
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}~\mathtt{w}
making
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
safe.

Since
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
is unsafe, we have
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\not\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})^{+}
and hence,
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime},\mathtt{w})\not\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})^{+}
.
Thus
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime})
is also unsafe, while clearly
\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime}\not\in\operatorname{InactiveWr}
.

∎

See
4.7

Proof.

Consider any
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})
-cycle
C
that traverses only write events connected by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
and
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
.
By construction, whenever
(\mathtt{w}_{1},\mathtt{w}_{2})\in\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
we also have
\mathtt{w}_{1}\preceq\mathtt{w}_{2}
.
This fact, in conjunction with
Lemma

4.6
implies that
C
is monotonic.
Since
C
is a cycle, it follows that it contains events of the same phase and step.
A direct inspection of each instantiation of each gadget concludes that no such cycle
C
can exist.

In particular, regarding the copy gadgets
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
and copy-down gadgets
\operatorname{\overline{Copy}}^{i}_{j}
, we have the following.

(1)

Observe that
C
does not cross
t_{3}
as this thread has no writes.

(2)

In turn,
C
does not cross any of
f_{1}
,
f_{4}
,
g_{1}
and
g_{4}
, as each of these threads can only enter
t_{3}
.

(3)

In turn,
C
does not cross any of
f_{2}
and
f_{3}
, as each of these threads can only enter
t_{3}
and
f_{1}
.
Similarly,
C
does not cross any of
f_{5}
and
f_{6}
, as each of these threads can only enter
t_{3}
and
f_{4}
.
Likewise,
C
does not cross any of
g_{2}
and
g_{3}
, as each of these threads can only enter
t_{3}
and
g_{1}
.
Similarly,
C
does not cross any of
g_{5}
and
g_{6}
, as each of these threads can only enter
t_{3}
and
g_{4}
.

(4)

In turn,
C
does not cross
t_{6}
, as this thread can only enter
f_{3}
,
f_{6}
,
g_{3}
and
g_{6}
.

(5)

Finally,
C
does not cross any of
t_{4}
and
t_{5}
, as these threads can only enter
t_{6}
, the
f
threads and the
g
threads.

Having excluded the above threads,
C
must be contained in
\{t_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[2]}
,
\{h_{c}\}_{c\in[3]}
,
q
and
p
.
Note that this implies that
C
only consists of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
/
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
-edges.
A straightforward inspection of the at-most-one-true and at-least-one-true gadgets (see
Fig.

10
and
Fig.

11
) shows that no such cycle exists.
Thus
(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})
is acyclic, as desired.
∎
