\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime}
is, without loss of generality monotonic, while the last two edges of
P
are also monotonic by construction (
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
-edges are always monotonic, while the only non-monotonic
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-edges go from writes to reads).
Hence
P
is monotonic.

On the other hand, we have
\mathtt{w}\preceq\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
(as
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
), while, by construction,
\mathtt{r}\preceq\mathtt{w}
.
Hence
\mathtt{r}\preceq\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
, and since
P
is monotonic, it must contain only events of the same phase and step.
In particular,
P
must be contained in one of the gadgets in
Figs.

8
,
9
,
10
and
11
.

The absence of such paths
P
can then be established by a careful inspection of the gadgets.
The statement is then followed by a case-by-case analysis of each write
\mathtt{w}
in each thread and each gadget.
In particular:

(1)

For the threads
t_{3}
and
t_{6}
the statement follows trivially, as these contain no writes.

(2)

For all writes on threads
\{f_{\ell},g_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[6]}
,
\{h_{c}\}_{c\in[3]}
,
p
and
q
, the statement follows by simply observing each of the possible instantiations of each gadget.

(3)

For all writes on threads
t_{4}
and
t_{5}
, it again suffices to observe each of the possible instantiations of each gadget separately, as
(i) the writes on
{\color[rgb]{0.9258,0.0625,0.1836}y_{6}}
/
{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}y_{8}}
are read by the
f
threads,
(ii) the writes on
{\color[rgb]{0.9258,0.0625,0.1836}z_{6}}
/
{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}z_{8}}
are read by the
g
threads, and
(iii) there are no
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-paths between the
f
threads and the
g
threads.

(4)

Finally, for the threads
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, we have to consider all writes of a given phase and step that appear in different gadgets (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},y_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.5781,0.0625}\mathtt{w}(t_{1},z_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
etc.).
The statement follows by the fact that all these writes appear in
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
following the predefined order
\sigma
on memory locations, and they are read in the same order by
t_{3}
.

∎

See
4.9

Proof.

Observe that
\operatorname{phase}(\mathtt{w})=\operatorname{phase}(\mathtt{r})
and
\operatorname{step}(\mathtt{w})=\operatorname{step}(\mathtt{r})
for all such triplets.
If
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\prec\mathtt{w}
, then by construction we have
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{w})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo})^{+}
, thus the triplet is safe.
On the other hand, if
\mathtt{w}\prec\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
, then also
\mathtt{r}\prec\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
(as
\mathtt{r}\preceq\mathtt{w}
by construction).
We argue that in this case there is no
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-path
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to12.34pt{\vbox to10.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.16943pt\lower-5.6833pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Due to

4.6
, we have
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\preceq\mathtt{w}^{\prime\prime}
and thus also
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\preceq\mathtt{r}^{\prime\prime}
.
Since
\mathtt{r}\prec\mathtt{w}^{\prime}
, we have
\mathtt{r}\prec\mathtt{r}^{\prime\prime}
.
Observe that this can only happen if
\mathtt{r}={\color[rgb]{0.9258,0.0625,0.1836}\mathtt{r}(g_{1},z_{6},v^{i}_{j})}
or
\mathtt{r}={\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}\mathtt{r}(g_{4},z_{8},v^{i}_{j})}
, for some
i\in[m]
and
j\in[n]
.
However, the only thread that writes to
{\color[rgb]{0.9258,0.0625,0.1836}z_{6}}
is
t_{4}
, and the only thread that writes to
{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}z_{8}}

.
In both cases we have
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
, and by
Lemma

4.8
, we have
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
.

Finally, we consider triplets where
\operatorname{phase}(\mathtt{w})=\operatorname{phase}(\mathtt{w}^{\prime})=\operatorname{phase}(\mathtt{r})
and
\operatorname{step}(\mathtt{w})=\operatorname{step}(\mathtt{w}^{\prime})=\operatorname{step}(\mathtt{r})
.
Note that all events of such a triplet must exist together in one of the gadgets in

8
,
9
,
10
and
11
.
Moreover, again due to
Lemma

4.6
, it suffices to only consider
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-paths
\mathtt{w}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}}\mathtt{r}
that traverse events in the same phase and step.
The lemma then follows by a laborious but straightforward case-by-base analysis of all triplets appearing in each of the gadgets.
We outline this analysis now.

(1)

For every triplet
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{r},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})
accessing a location that is written by a single thread (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.9375,0.5938,0.1758}y_{3}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}y_{4}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9258,0.0625,0.1836}y_{6}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}y_{8}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9375,0.5938,0.1758}z_{3}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1172,0.5664,1}z_{4}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9258,0.0625,0.1836}z_{6}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.125,0.5586,0.7188}z_{8}}
), we have
(\mathtt{w},\mathtt{w}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
and thus
(\mathtt{w}^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
by

4.8
.

(2)

Consider the read
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
(see

).
Regardless of which thread it reads from (i.e., thread

if

or thread

if
s_{j}=\top
), the write in the opposite thread does not have an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-path to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},x_{1},v^{i}_{j})}
that is contained in phase
i
and step
j
.
Similarly for the read
{\color[rgb]{0.545,0.545,0.545}\mathtt{r}(t_{6},x_{2},v^{i}_{j})}
.

(3)

Consider the read
{\color[rgb]{0.2969,0.1914,0.6211}\mathtt{r}(f_{1},y_{5},v^{i}_{j})}
in
\operatorname{Copy}^{i}_{j}
(see

).
Regardless of which thread it reads from (i.e., thread

if
s_{j}=\perp
or thread

if

), the write in the opposite thread does not have an
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-path to
{\color[rgb]{0.2969,0.1914,0.6211}\mathtt{r}(f_{1},y_{5},v^{i}_{j})}

and step

.
Similarly for the read

in the copy-down gadget

(see
Fig.

9
).

(4)

).

) we have

and

.

(5)

Consider the read
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})}
in the at-most-one-true gadget
\operatorname{AMOne}[c]^{i}_{j,k}
(see
Fig.

10
).
If
s_{j}=\top\!\!\!\!\top
(see
Fig.

10(b)
), we have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
and
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}}
, which also implies
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(h_{c},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}\cup\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}mo}})^{+}
.
Moreover, all other writes to
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}
are of a different step.
On the other hand, if
s_{j}=\perp
(see
Figs.

10(c)
and
10(d)
), we have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}rf}
.
Observe that in this case, all other writes on
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}a_{c}}
are of a different step,
except
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})}
.
Due to
Lemma

4.8
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{w}(t_{2},a_{c},\overline{v}^{i}_{j})},{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{j})})\not\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
.
A similar analysis holds for
{\color[rgb]{0.9648,0.1406,0.5625}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},a_{c},v^{i}_{k})}
, as well as the reads
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{j})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{k})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathtt{r}(t_{3},b,v^{i}_{\ell})}
in the at-least-one-gadget
\operatorname{ALOne}^{i}_{j,k,\ell}
(see
Fig.

11
).

∎

, as no write is read by a read in the same thread.

in

after

. Clearly such an

and

culminates in
\mathtt{r}
with
\mathsf{tid}(\mathtt{r})=t
.
Then it must be that
(e^{\prime},\mathtt{r})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
: if not, we have
(\mathtt{r},e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
, and this results in the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}hb}
-cycle

e^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox to12.34pt{\vbox to10.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.16943pt\lower-5.6833pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

B.2.
Proofs from
Section

5.3

For the consistency problem on an abstract execution
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}=(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po})
,
the semantics of
\mathsf{TSO}
can be represented operationally as a labeled transition system
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
.
A state in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
is a triplet
\langle\operatorname{P},\operatorname{B},\operatorname{M}\rangle
:

(1)

\operatorname{P}\subseteq\mathsf{E}
is the set of events that have been executed so far.

(2)

\operatorname{B}\colon\mathcal{T}\to(\mathsf{W})^{*}
maps every thread
t
to a sequence of writes
\mathtt{w}(t,x_{1}),\mathtt{w}(t,x_{2}),\dots,\mathtt{w}(t,x_{i})
, which represents the state of the buffer of thread
t
.

(3)

\operatorname{M}\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathsf{W}
maps every memory location of the shared memory to the most recent write to it.

Given a set
\operatorname{P}\subseteq\mathsf{E}
, an event
e
is
enabled
in
\operatorname{P}
, written
\operatorname{enbl}(\operatorname{P},e)
, if
e(t,x)\not\in\operatorname{P}
and every
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
-predecessor
e^{\prime}
of
e
(i.e.,
(e^{\prime},e)\in\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
) is such that
e^{\prime}\in\operatorname{P}
.

Fig.

14
lists the transitions in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
, which are of the following types.

(1)

{\small[\textsc{Buffer Write}]}
defines the execution of a write
\mathtt{w}(t,x,v)
, which modifies the buffer of
t
.

(2)

{\small[\textsc{Memory Write}]}
defines the flushing of a buffer write
\mathtt{w}(t,x,v)
to the shared memory.

(3)

{\small[\textsc{Buffer Read}]}
defines the execution of a read
\mathtt{r}(t,x,v)
, reading from the buffer of
t
.

(4)

{\small[\textsc{Memory Read}]}
defines the execution of a read
\mathtt{r}(t,x,v)
, reading from the shared memory.

Naturally,
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}\models\mathsf{TSO}
iff a state
\langle\mathsf{E},\operatorname{B},\operatorname{M}\rangle
is reachable from the initial state
\langle\varnothing,\lambda t.\epsilon,\lambda x.0\rangle
in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
.

\displaystyle{\small[\textsc{Buffer Write}]}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{gathered}\operatorname{enbl}(\operatorname{P},\mathtt{w}(t,x,v))\\
\operatorname{P}^{\prime}=\operatorname{P}\cup\{\mathtt{w}(t,x,v)\}\\
\operatorname{B}^{\prime}=\operatorname{B}[t\mapsto\operatorname{B}(t)\cdot\mathtt{w}(t,x,v)]\end{gathered}}{\langle\operatorname{P},\operatorname{B},\operatorname{M}\rangle\xrightarrow{\mathtt{w}(t,x,v)}\langle\operatorname{P}^{\prime},\operatorname{B}^{\prime},\operatorname{M}\rangle}

\displaystyle{\small[\textsc{Memory Write}]}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{gathered}\operatorname{B}(t)=\mathtt{w}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{w}_{i}\\
\operatorname{B}^{\prime}=\operatorname{B}[t\mapsto\mathtt{w}_{2},\dots,\mathtt{w}_{i}]\\
\operatorname{M}^{\prime}=\operatorname{M}[x\mapsto\mathtt{w}_{1}]\end{gathered}}{\langle\operatorname{P},\operatorname{B},\operatorname{M}\rangle\xrightarrow{b(t)}\langle\operatorname{P},\operatorname{B}^{\prime},\operatorname{M}^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle{\small[\textsc{Buffer Read}]}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{gathered}\operatorname{enbl}(\operatorname{P},\mathtt{r}(t,x,v))\\
\operatorname{P}^{\prime}=\operatorname{P}\cup\{\mathtt{r}(t,x,v)\}\\
\operatorname{B}(t)=\mathtt{w}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{w}_{i}\\
\exists j\in[i].\mathtt{w}_{j}=\mathtt{w}(t,x,v)\text{ and }\forall\ell>j,\mathtt{w}_{\ell}\neq\mathtt{w}(t,x,u)\end{gathered}}{\langle\operatorname{P},\operatorname{B},\operatorname{M}\rangle\xrightarrow{\mathtt{r}(t,x,v)}\langle\operatorname{P}^{\prime},\operatorname{B},\operatorname{M}\rangle}

\displaystyle{\small[\textsc{Memory Read}]}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{gathered}\operatorname{enbl}(\operatorname{P},\mathtt{r}(t,x,v))\\
\operatorname{P}^{\prime}=\operatorname{P}\cup\{\mathtt{r}(t,x,v)\}\\
\operatorname{B}(t)=\mathtt{w}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{w}_{i}\\
\not\exists j\in[i].\mathtt{w}_{j}=\mathtt{w}(t,x,\cdot)\\
\operatorname{M}(x)=\mathtt{w}(f,x,v)\end{gathered}}{\langle\operatorname{P},\operatorname{B},\operatorname{M}\rangle\xrightarrow{\mathtt{r}(t,x,v)}\langle\operatorname{P}^{\prime},\operatorname{B},\operatorname{M}\rangle}

Figure 14.

The operational semantics of TSO.

See
1.4

Proof.

We only argue about
\mathsf{TSO}
, as the case of
\mathsf{PSO}
is similar.
Our proof is by bounding the size of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
by a polynomial (in the size of
\overline{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}
) bound.
Indeed:

(1)

Since
\operatorname{P}
is downward closed for
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
, there are
\leq n^{\kappa}
different instantiations of
\operatorname{P}
.

(2)

For every thread
t
,
\operatorname{B}(t)
is a contiguous sequence of writes of thread
t
.
Hence there are
\leq n^{2}
different instantiations of
\operatorname{B}(t)
, leading to
\leq n^{2\cdot\kappa}
different instantiations of
\operatorname{B}
.

(3)

For every memory location
x
and given an instantiation of
\operatorname{P}
and
\operatorname{B}
,
\operatorname{M}(x)
can hold
\leq\kappa
different writes, one from each thread
t
, which is uniquely determined as the latest (wrt
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,0.2}po}
) write of thread
t
on
x
that is in
\operatorname{P}
but not in
\operatorname{B}(t)
.
Hence, given an instantiation of
\operatorname{P}
and
\operatorname{B}
, there are
\leq\kappa^{d}
instantiations of
\operatorname{M}
.

It follows from the above that the number of states of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{TSO}}
is bounded by
\kappa^{d}\cdot n^{O(\kappa^{2})}
, and thus polynomial when
\kappa,d=O(1)
.
∎

Finally, we remark that
Theorem

1.4
also holds in the presence of fences and atomic read-modify-write (RMW) events, by a very similar argument.
